False positives only: 'nonstop_tsc' (Ch. 17), NaN 'poisoning' (Ch. 27), a 'poison symbol' (Ch. 74 ex. 6), the stack-protector 'canary' row (Ch. 72 §72.3), a stack guard-page fault (Ch. 23 §23.5) and 'no per-object guard pages' named as the *loss* in Ch. 72 §72.4.2. Opened Ch. 5 in full: §5.1, §5.2, §5.5 ('Attaching gdb to the live hot path') and the §5.6 checklist all prescribe 'never attach... debug a replay'; §5.4.2 covers hardware watchpoints (`watch`) explicitly 'in a replay/test (never the live hot path)'; §5.4.3 offers only bpftrace, the Ch. 71 binary logger and PMU counters for live observation. Ch. 72 §72.4.2 develops prevention (generation counters/tagged handles, clear-on-free in debug builds, ASan over the arena in test) and §72.3's hardening table has no hardened-libstdc++/libc++ row. Ch. 40 §40.5 and Ch. 72 §72.3 both say sanitizers are not run in production, without offering the production alternative.

## G18 — Record/replay limits and replay-divergence forensics: what rr cannot record (kernel bypass, DMA, multi-core timing), alternatives, per-event state digests, bisecting to the first diverging input, determinism linting of the core

- **Theme:** B. Debugging & diagnostics · **Proposed kind:** expand-existing-chapter · **Sources:** debug-diag#4, debug-diag#9
- **Verdict:** partially-covered · **Priority:** high (was medium)
- **Why this priority:** Author-requested area, and the book's prescribed architecture (deterministic core + replay gate, Ch. 74–76) names replay divergence as its failure mode in four places while giving no way to localise it, so the CI gate it prescribes is unactionable when it fails; the rr block also corrects an overclaim about the recommended debugger on kernel-bypass code.
- **Refined placement:** Two insertions, no new chapter. (a) Ch. 5 §5.4.2: a 'What rr cannot record, and what to use instead' block (~300–400 words plus a comparison table: rr / Pernosco / Undo LiveRecorder / the deterministic core as the recorder) — hardware-DMA'd memory (ef_vi/DPDK rings, Ch. 62), shared memory written by peers outside the trace (Ch. 50), single-core serialization of a Ch. 37 pipeline (timing bugs change; chaos mode `-h`), the retired-conditional-branch counter requirement and `perf_event_paranoid`/VM/AArch64 caveats, trapped `rdtsc`/`cpuid` (Ch. 17); soften §5.3's 'low-overhead single-core recording', add the §5.5 pitfall, refine the Appendix F entry. (b) Ch. 74: new technique subsection 'Divergence forensics and determinism auditing' (as §74.4.6, or §74.4.7 if the watchdog-snapshot section proposed in another batch is adopted): per-event state digest journaled beside outputs (tie the Ch. 75 §75.4.2/§75.4.4 record format), first-diverging-event localisation by diffing digest streams, checkpoint-based bisection over the input sequence, a 'stop at first divergence' replay mode that drops into gdb/rr (→ §5.4.2), and a determinism lint (clock, random, pointer-keyed hashing under ASLR, unordered iteration, uninitialised padding bytes, FP reassociation — tie Ch. 27 §27.4.3). Cross-reference from Ch. 76 §76.4.1 and §76.5.
- **What is missing / scope notes:** The covered angle is the pattern and its failure mode; the method is missing. Keep the digest cheap and measured (incremental hash of mutated fields vs periodic full-state hash, cost per event on the book-update loop) and state the journal-format implication (a digest column). Pointer-keyed hash maps under ASLR and uninitialised padding (Ch. 9) in journaled structs deserve explicit treatment because the book's own containers (Ch. 25) and binary journals (Ch. 71/75) are exposed to both.
- **Closest existing coverage:**
  - 05-debugging-low-latency-optimized-code.md §5.3 and §5.4.2 (rr record/replay, reverse-continue — as the gold standard, no limits)
  - 74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.5 ('A non-deterministic "deterministic" core' — causes in one sentence) and §74.6 ex. 2 ('Prove determinism')
  - 75-capture-persistence-replay-storage.md §75.5 ('Non-deterministic replay')
  - 76-production-profiling-end-to-end-case-study.md §76.4.1 (determinism gate), §76.4.2 ('Bisect regressions with replay' — across builds), §76.5
  - appendix-F-glossary.md ('rr' and 'Deterministic state machine' entries)
- **Evidence (greps and reads):** Ran: grep -n -i -E '\brr\b|rr record|chaos mode|Pernosco|UndoDB|LiveRecorder|perf_event_paranoid|state digest|state hash|hash the state|first diverg|diverg|bisect|golden|nondetermin|non-determin|reverse-continue|time-travel|record/replay' /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/*.md, then 'golden|determinis' over Ch. 5/74/75/76. `rr` appears only in Ch. 4's teaser, Ch. 5 (§5.3, §5.4.2, §5.6 ex. 2 and checklist, §5.7/§5.8) and Appendix F — always as 'the gold-standard technique' with 'low-overhead single-core recording'; no limitations, 0 hits for chaos mode / Pernosco / Undo / LiveRecorder. `perf_event_paranoid` appears once (Ch. 2 §2.5, perf permissions), not in the rr context. 'diverg' hits: Ch. 74 §74.5 ('you discover it when a replay diverges'), §74.6 ex. 2 ('sneak a clock_gettime into the core and watch replay diverge'), Ch. 75 §75.1/§75.5 ('Non-deterministic replay'), Ch. 76 §76.4.1 ('Any divergence is a behavior change') and §76.5 — all name the failure mode, none localises it. 'bisect' hits are Ch. 76 §76.4.2 and ex. 4 — bisecting across builds/commits, not across the input sequence. 'state hash|state digest|checksum' → no relevant hits (the only 'checksum' is the Ch. 2 demo variable). 'golden' → the CI fixture only. Ch. 74 §74.5 lists causes in one sentence (clock, random seed, 'map iteration order or thread scheduling', I/O) plus 'Hidden side effects' (static counters, errno, singletons); no audit/lint method.

## G19 — Unwinding and stack-capture cost, and the standard diagnostics toolkit: frame pointers vs DWARF CFI vs LBR vs SFrame, libunwind/backtrace(), std::source_location, std::stacktrace, C++26 <debugging> and contracts

- **Theme:** B. Debugging & diagnostics · **Proposed kind:** new-section-in-existing-chapter · **Sources:** debug-diag#5, debug-diag#12, cpp-features#11
- **Verdict:** partially-covered · **Priority:** medium
- **Why this priority:** Valuable and in requested areas (debugging-diagnostics / language-features) — it fixes an unmeasured hot-path claim and fills obvious Appendix H holes — but the existing recommendation is actionable without it, so it is not an operating-blocking gap.
- **Refined placement:** Split as proposed, one section per host chapter. (a) Ch. 22 new §22.4.4 'Debug info, frame pointers and unwind tables: what the production binary carries' — measured `-fno-omit-frame-pointer`/`-momit-leaf-frame-pointer` cost on the decode and book-update kernels; per-frame cost of frame-pointer walk vs `.eh_frame`/libunwind (`_Unwind_Backtrace`) vs LBR vs SFrame; `-fasynchronous-unwind-tables` with `-fno-exceptions`; `-g`/`-g1`/`-gline-tables-only`/`-gsplit-dwarf`/`-gz`/debuginfod and archiving; Appendix D.4/D.7 rows. (b) Ch. 20 new §20.4.4 'Assertions, contracts and the diagnostics library on the hot path' — `std::source_location` as the C++20 replacement for `__FILE__`/`__LINE__` in the Ch. 71 site table (verified as immediates in §20.5), `std::stacktrace` with a `pmr` resource on the rare error path (Ch. 24) and deferred symbolization, `<debugging>` (`std::breakpoint_if_debugging`, `is_debugger_present`), C++26 contracts and enforcement modes, `std::unreachable`/`[[assume]]`; rows in Appendix H.2/H.3. Cross-reference from Ch. 2 §2.4.2, Ch. 5 §5.4.1 and Ch. 71 §71.4.2.
- **What is missing / scope notes:** The gap is rigor and completeness rather than a missing capability: the frame-pointer/unwinder recommendation exists but unmeasured, and the standard diagnostics features are absent from the feature matrix whose job is to list them. Make the Ch. 71 logger use `std::source_location` as its C++20 default (macro fallback), since that is the book's own motivating use. Keep SFrame brief (kernel/binutils status) and state toolchain availability per Appendix H conventions.
- **Closest existing coverage:**
  - 02-measure-first-profiling-pmu.md §2.4.2 and §2.5 (frame-pointer vs DWARF vs LBR recommendation, unmeasured)
  - 22-build-toolchain-for-speed.md §22.5/§22.6 ('Keep frame pointers (small cost)')
  - appendix-D-compiler-flag-reference.md D.4 (`-fno-omit-frame-pointer` row)
  - appendix-I-tooling-cookbook.md I.1 (`--call-graph fp|dwarf|lbr` one-liners)
  - 05-debugging-low-latency-optimized-code.md §5.4.1/§5.5 (`-g` is codegen-neutral; `-gsplit-dwarf`, `objcopy --only-keep-debug`)
  - 20-the-cost-of-abstractions.md §20.2/§20.5 (`.eh_frame`/LSDA unwinding tables for exceptions)
  - 71-zero-overhead-logging.md §71.4.2 (format-site IDs via macro/consteval — the natural `std::source_location` use)
- **Evidence (greps and reads):** Ran: grep -n -i -E 'libunwind|asynchronous-unwind|unwind-tables|SFrame|gsplit-dwarf|debuginfod|std::stacktrace|<stacktrace>|stacktrace|source_location|<debugging>|breakpoint_if_debugging|is_debugger_present|std::breakpoint|contract_assert|P2900|frame pointer|frame-pointer|omit-frame-pointer|\.eh_frame|_Unwind|backtrace|__builtin_return_address|\bORC\b|gline-tables|-g1\b|-gz\b|dwarf' /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/*.md, plus 'source_location|stacktrace|debugging|contract|unreachable|assume' in appendix-H and 'assert|unreachable|contract|NDEBUG' in Ch. 20. Zero hits for libunwind, -fasynchronous-unwind-tables, SFrame, _Unwind_*, backtrace( (the only 'backtrace' is gdb `bt` in Ch. 5 §5.4.2), std::stacktrace, std::source_location, <debugging>, breakpoint_if_debugging, contract_assert, -gline-tables-only, -g1, -gz; Appendix H has no row for any of them (its only diagnostics-adjacent row is `[[assume]]`). Frame pointers are recommended without measurement in Ch. 2 §2.3 build line and §2.4.2 ('Build with -fno-omit-frame-pointer (or rely on DWARF/LBR unwinding)'), §2.5 ('prefer LBR/frame-pointer unwinding over DWARF on the hot path'), Ch. 22 §22.5/§22.6 ('Keep frame pointers (small cost)'), Appendix D.4 ('Costs one register (negligible...)'), Appendix I.1 (`--call-graph fp|dwarf|lbr` with 'slower'/'cheapest, shallow' notes) — contrary to the book's 'every performance claim is backed by a measurement' rule. `-gsplit-dwarf`/`objcopy --only-keep-debug` are covered in Ch. 5 §5.4.1/§5.5 and Appendix F. `.eh_frame`/LSDA are developed in Ch. 20 §20.2/§20.5 for throw cost and binary size, not as a stack-capture mechanism; Ch. 20 §20.4 has no assertion/contract/std::unreachable material. Ch. 71 §71.4.2 describes format-site IDs 'via a macro + a static registration, or a consteval mechanism' without naming std::source_location.

## G20 — Debugging the hardware path: in-fabric logic analyzers and stage timestamps, NIC/PCIe health counters, correlating tap captures with host traces

- **Theme:** B. Debugging & diagnostics · **Proposed kind:** new-section-in-existing-chapter · **Sources:** debug-diag#10
- **Verdict:** partially-covered · **Priority:** medium
- **Why this priority:** Specialised: the FPGA-internal tooling is marginal to a C++-on-Linux book, while the useful parts (tap↔host alignment, NIC/PCIe health counters) are better served inside the G23 tracing chapter and the NIC/PCIe chapters than as a standalone FPGA section.
- **Refined placement:** Do not make Ch. 69 the home for all of it; split by audience. (a) Ch. 69 new §69.4.4 'Debugging and observing the fabric path' — short (~500 words): in-fabric stage timestamps exported in a trailer/side channel, ILA/SignalTap triggers keyed on a sequence number, simulation vs hardware-in-the-loop against captured pcaps (Ch. 75), replacing the §69.5 clause. (b) The per-packet ledger aligning tap pcap → NIC RX hardware stamp → first host touch (TSC↔PHC) → app stage stamps → TX hardware stamp → tap egress (with `tshark -T fields -e frame.time_epoch`, pcap-ng interface timestamps, a sequence-number join key) belongs in the G23 in-band tracing chapter (or Ch. 76 §76.3 if that chapter is not adopted) — it is the production budget-attribution method, not an FPGA topic. (c) NIC/PCIe health counters as diagnostics (`ethtool -S` beyond drops, `devlink health`, vendor tools `onload_stackdump`/`exanic-config`, PCIe AER/`CorrErr`/link-retrain counters, `/sys/bus/pci/.../aer_dev_*`) belong in Ch. 55 §55.5–55.6 / Ch. 62 §62.5 ('own monitoring') and Ch. 66 §66.5 with Appendix I.8 one-liners — or in the OS/network health-monitoring material proposed in another batch.
- **What is missing / scope notes:** Only the `ethtool -S` drop counters and the cross-timestamping bridge are developed today. The genuinely new and broadly useful pieces are the tap↔host alignment method and the NIC/PCIe health-counter inventory; FPGA-internal debug tooling (ILA) is a hardware engineer's domain and should stay a paragraph. Give the attribution split (fabric vs NIC-ingress→DMA vs DMA→first-touch (DDIO/NUMA, Ch. 56/16) vs decode→decision vs TX-enqueue→wire) one measured example.
- **Closest existing coverage:**
  - 55-nic-features-offloads.md §55.5/§55.6 and appendix-I-tooling-cookbook.md I.8 (`ethtool -S` drop counters — developed)
  - 58-clock-synchronization-ptp.md §58.4.1 (TSC↔PHC cross-timestamping) and §58.4.2 (per-hop hardware-timestamp decomposition)
  - 60-network-fabric-switching.md §60.4.3 (fabric timestamping, mirror/SPAN, aggregation taps)
  - 75-capture-persistence-replay-storage.md §75.4.1 (tap/mirror capture with PTP timestamps)
  - 69-fpga-acceleration.md §69.3 and §69.5 (one clause each: 'external timing tap', 'signal taps, simulation')
  - 62-kernel-bypass-userspace-networking.md §62.5 ('Losing kernel tooling')
  - 66-pcie-host-device-boundary.md §66.5 (`lspci -vv` link width/speed)
- **Evidence (greps and reads):** Ran: grep -n -i -E '\bILA\b|ChipScope|SignalTap|logic analy|tshark|wireshark|exanic-capture|exanic-config|onload_stackdump|onload_tcpdump|solar_capture|devlink|per-packet ledger|ledger|\bAER\b|PCIe error|lspci -vv|ethtool -S|rx_dropped|rx_missed|correlat|PTP_SYS_OFFSET|SOF_TIMESTAMPING|pcap|\btap\b|mirror port|SPAN port|stage timestamp|simulation|signal tap|testbench|hardware-in-the-loop' over Ch. 55/58/60/62/66/69/70/75/76 and Appendix I, and 'telemetry|health|counter|diagnos|debug|stackdump' over Ch. 55/62/70. Zero hits for ILA/ChipScope/SignalTap/logic analyzer, tshark/wireshark, exanic-*/onload_stackdump/solar_capture, devlink, AER/PCIe error counters, 'ledger'. Ch. 69 §69.3 says 'Measure with hardware timestamps ... or an external timing tap' and §69.5 says 'Debugging is hardware-level (signal taps, simulation)' and 'Verify rigorously (simulation, formal verification, hardware-in-the-loop)' — one clause each, no method. Developed elsewhere: `ethtool -S` rx_missed/rx_no_buffer drop counters (Ch. 55 §55.5/§55.6, Appendix I.8 with a recipe); TSC↔PHC cross-timestamping via `PTP_SYS_OFFSET_PRECISE` (Ch. 58 §58.4.1, Ch. 17 §17.4.2); fabric taps/mirror ports with PTP timestamps as the capture source (Ch. 60 §60.4.3, Ch. 75 §75.4.1); per-hop decomposition across hosts (Ch. 58 §58.4.2). Ch. 66 §66.5 has `lspci -vv` for link width only; Ch. 62 §62.5/§62.6 ex. 4 note bypass loses tcpdump/netstat and 'you must provide your own capture' with no vendor diagnostic tools; Ch. 70 mentions DPU 'telemetry (Ch. 61)' only as a placement decision. Nothing joins a tap pcap to host stage stamps or FPGA stage timestamps into one per-packet record.

## G21 — User-space tracing frameworks and unified timelines: LTTng-UST, uftrace, Perfetto/Tracy, ftrace integration and trace formats vs the in-house binary logger

- **Theme:** B. Debugging & diagnostics · **Proposed kind:** new-section-in-existing-chapter · **Sources:** debug-diag#11
- **Verdict:** genuinely-missing · **Priority:** low
- **Why this priority:** Marginal to the book's theme: it surveys alternatives to a logging/tracing stack the book already builds and measures (Ch. 61, 71); only the unified-timeline method adds operating value, and it fits in half a page.
- **Refined placement:** Not Ch. 61 (eBPF/XDP). Smallest footprint: a short subsection in Ch. 71 — §71.4.4 'Exporting the binary log to a unified timeline' — converting Ch. 71 records + `perf sched`/ftrace sched_switch/IRQ events + NIC hardware stamps to Perfetto/Chrome-JSON or CTF for incident forensics; LTTng-UST (per-CPU lock-free rings, flight-recorder/snapshot mode) as the off-the-shelf alternative with its per-event cost measured against the §71.3 number; uftrace/Tracy as replay-box tools in a paragraph. Alternatively a section of the G23 tracing chapter if that is adopted. Plus Appendix I.5 one-liners (`lttng create --snapshot`, `trace-cmd record -e sched`, `perf script` → Perfetto).
- **What is missing / scope notes:** The book deliberately builds its own logger and standardises on bpftrace; what is missing is (a) LTTng-UST's hot-path cost compared with the Ch. 71 logger, (b) flight-recorder mode as an incident tool, and (c) the single-timeline view (app records, scheduler, IRQs, NIC stamps). Keep the tool survey to a paragraph each; the value is the method.
- **Closest existing coverage:**
  - 61-ebpf-bpftrace-xdp.md §61.3–§61.4.1 (USDT/tracepoint/uprobe overhead and bpftrace — the book's chosen tracing stack; adjacent, not the topic)
  - 71-zero-overhead-logging.md §71.4.2 (in-house binary log + offline decoder — the format the proposal would export; adjacent)
  - 45-realtime-scheduling-kernel-tuning.md §45.6 ex. 5 and appendix-C-system-tuning-checklist.md C.5 (ftrace named in passing)
- **Evidence (greps and reads):** Ran: grep -n -i -E 'LTTng|uftrace|Perfetto|Tracy|babeltrace|\bCTF\b|trace_marker|Chrome JSON|chrome://tracing|unified timeline|single timeline|one timeline|ftrace|trace-cmd|KernelShark|function_graph|tracefs|finstrument-functions|\bUSDT\b|tracepoint|trace format|timeline' /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/*.md. LTTng, uftrace, Perfetto, Tracy, babeltrace, CTF, trace_marker, Chrome JSON, trace-cmd, KernelShark, function_graph, tracefs, -finstrument-functions, 'timeline' → 0 hits ('timeline' matches only 'timeliness' in Ch. 10 §10.2.2). 'ftrace' → two passing mentions: Ch. 45 §45.6 ex. 5 ('`cyclictest -b <us>` (break-trace) + ftrace') and Appendix C.5 ('`perf` / `ftrace` / the `hwlat` detector'). USDT/tracepoints are developed in Ch. 61 §61.3–§61.4.1 (probe-overhead table, bpftrace one-liners, BCC tools) and Appendix I.5; the in-house binary logger in Ch. 71 §71.4.2 (NanoLog model, offline decoder). Neither mentions a common trace format or merging application records with kernel scheduler/IRQ events and NIC stamps on one timeline; Ch. 76 §76.2 names 'eBPF/bpftrace low-overhead tracing' as the production tracing tool.

## G22 — Production profiling that is actually in production, and beyond the core PMU: perf sampling cost on an isolated core, rdpmc, LBR, Intel PT, continuous profilers; uncore PMUs (IMC/CHA/UPI/IIO), AMD IBS, RAPL and SMI events

- **Theme:** B. Debugging & diagnostics · **Proposed kind:** expand-existing-chapter · **Sources:** observability-depth#4, hardware-frontier#10
- **Verdict:** partially-covered · **Priority:** high
- **Why this priority:** Central and author-requested: a chapter titled 'Production Profiling' has no technique for profiling production, the sampling-cost rule is unmeasured against the book's own standard, and uncore/IBS/PT are the counters that attribute the tail events (DRAM bandwidth, DDIO, cross-socket, SMI) the rest of the book teaches readers to hunt.
- **Refined placement:** Two sections plus recipes, as the analysts proposed. (a) Ch. 2 new §2.4.4 'Beyond the core PMU': uncore PMUs (`uncore_imc` CAS→bandwidth, CHA/LLC, UPI for Ch. 16, IIO for the Ch. 56 DDIO path), AMD IBS (`perf record -e ibs_op//`, what `perf mem`/`c2c` rely on on Zen; TMA vs Zen pipeline-utilization metrics), RAPL/energy and frequency-license events (Ch. 29 AVX downclock), SMI counting (`msr` SMI_COUNT, `hwlat`), Intel PT as the exact control-flow recorder for a spike window; copy-paste recipes in Appendix I.2/I.3. (b) Ch. 76: reframe §76.2 into three tiers (always-on / targeted-live / offline-replay) and add §76.4.4 'Profiling the live system safely': the hot thread's p99.9 measured with `perf record` attached at 99/999/9999 Hz and fp vs DWARF vs LBR unwinding, the IPIs `-C` sends to an isolated/nohz_full core, NMI + PEBS-drain cost, `perf_event_open` + mmap'd page + `rdpmc` for syscall-free per-stage counter reads, LBR snapshots on a tail event, Intel PT windows, continuous-profiler overhead budgets; add an Appendix E row for 'perf sample (NMI + stack walk)'. Cross-reference from Ch. 2 §2.5, Ch. 5 §5.4.3 and Ch. 61 §61.3.
- **What is missing / scope notes:** The book has the rule ('≤ a few kHz, prefer LBR/fp') without the measurement and a hard two-tier stance that leaves no sanctioned way to profile the live box; both halves of the proposal are missing beyond names. Keep the AMD material first-class, tie uncore IIO/CHA to Ch. 56 (DDIO misses) and UPI to Ch. 16, and present `rdpmc` as the always-on per-stage instrument alongside the Ch. 17 TSC stamp.
- **Closest existing coverage:**
  - 02-measure-first-profiling-pmu.md §2.2.3 (sampling vs counting, PEBS), §2.4.2 (fp/DWARF/LBR unwinding), §2.4.3 (VTune; 'AMD uProf' named), §2.5 ('The observer effect' rule of thumb; `perf_event_paranoid`)
  - 61-ebpf-bpftrace-xdp.md §61.3 (probe-overhead table) and §61.4.1 (Parca/Pyroscope named)
  - 76-production-profiling-end-to-end-case-study.md §76.2 (two-tier always-on vs offline model) and §76.5 ('Profiling overhead distorting the measurement')
  - 16-numa-architecture.md §16.3 (uncore/offcore remote-DRAM events, one sentence)
  - 06-system-setup-for-low-latency.md §6.4.1, 45-realtime-scheduling-kernel-tuning.md §45.5, appendix-C-system-tuning-checklist.md C.1/C.5 (SMIs as jitter; `hwlat`)
  - appendix-I-tooling-cookbook.md I.1 (`--call-graph lbr`) and I.9 (`-j any,u`)
- **Evidence (greps and reads):** Ran: grep -n -i -E 'rdpmc|Intel PT|Processor Trace|intel_pt|\bLBR\b|last.branch|perf record -C|perf -C|sampling overhead|sampling frequency|-F [0-9]+|uncore|\bIMC\b|\bCHA\b|\bUPI\b|\bIIO\b|\bIBS\b|ibs_op|RAPL|energy|\bSMI\b|rdmsr|continuous profil|Parca|Pyroscope|perf top|PEBS|\bNMI\b|uProf|perf_event_open|observer effect|always-on' over Ch. 2/5/16/33/44/56/61/76 and Appendices E/I, then '\bSMI\b|SMIs|hwlat|uncore|offcore' over all chapters. rdpmc → 0; Intel PT / Processor Trace → 0; IBS/ibs_op → 0 (AMD appears only as 'the analogous tool is AMD uProf', Ch. 2 §2.4.3); RAPL → one hit as a BIOS power-limit knob (Ch. 6 §6.4.1), never as a counter; SMI → named as a jitter source (Ch. 6, Ch. 45 §45.4/§45.5/ex. 5, Appendix C.1/C.5 with `hwlat`) with no SMI_COUNT/`msr` recipe; uncore → one sentence in Ch. 16 §16.3 ('the uncore `UNC_M_*`/offcore-response remote events') plus Ch. 56 references; CHA/UPI/IIO as PMUs → 0 (UPI appears only as the interconnect name). perf_event_open → named as the syscall in Ch. 2 §2.2.1 only. LBR → Ch. 2 §2.4.2/§2.5 and Appendix I.1 (`--call-graph lbr`, 'cheapest, shallow') and I.9 (`-j any,u` for BOLT) — as an unwinder, never as a last-branch spike recorder. Sampling cost: Ch. 2 §2.5 'The observer effect' ('Keep sampling rates sane (≤ a few kHz), prefer LBR/frame-pointer unwinding over DWARF') — a rule of thumb with no numbers and nothing on `-C` against an isolated/nohz_full core or NMI/PEBS-drain cost. Ch. 61 §61.3 measures probe overhead per type and §61.4.1 names Parca/Pyroscope. Ch. 76 §76.2 is a two-tier model ('PRODUCTION (always-on, ~free)' vs 'OFFLINE / CI (deep, expensive)') stating 'Deep analysis happens offline, on replays'; §76.5 'Profiling overhead distorting the measurement' repeats it. No technique anywhere for profiling the live box between those tiers.

## G23 — In-band latency tracing: per-hop timestamps and correlation IDs carried through rings and processes, budget attribution in production, and exchange-provided timestamps (TransactTime/SendingTime/TrdRegTS) for attributing the budget between you, the wire and the matching engine

- **Theme:** B. Debugging & diagnostics · **Proposed kind:** new-chapter · **Sources:** observability-depth#1, observability-depth#5
- **Verdict:** partially-covered · **Priority:** high
- **Why this priority:** Central and author-requested: Ch. 76's production-observation model and tick-to-trade table assume a per-stage, cross-process timing mechanism the book never builds, and venue-provided timestamps are the only clock that tells a desk whether lost races are its own latency, the wire, or the matching engine.
- **Refined placement:** A new chapter in Part XII immediately after Ch. 71 is justified: the mechanism is chapter-sized by the book's conventions and Ch. 76 (the 3.4k-word capstone) cannot absorb it. Working title 'In-Band Latency Tracing & Budget Attribution': fixed-size per-message trace header (venue sequence number / ClOrdID as correlation ID + a vector of `rdtscp` stage stamps) carried through the Ch. 34/37/50 rings vs out-of-band side tables; the stamp and extra-cache-line cost measured on the Ch. 34 SPSC ring; contention-free ID allocation and the causal tick→decision→order link across the Ch. 74 processes; off-path per-stage histogram aggregation (Ch. 3/71); a section on exchange-provided timestamps (CME MDP 3.0 `SendingTime`/`TransactTime`, iLink 3 `SendingTimeEpoch`/`RequestTime`, Eurex EOBI `TransactTime`/ETI `TrdRegTSTimeIn/Out`, Nasdaq ITCH/OUCH nanoseconds-since-midnight) with the one-way-feed-latency and gateway-in/matching-engine-in attribution method; and the tap↔host per-packet ledger from G20. Then Ch. 58 §58.4.2's bullet becomes a cross-reference paragraph (no separate §58.4.4), Appendix J.5 gains the field-level timestamp table, Appendix F gains entries, and Ch. 76 §76.2/§76.3 reference the mechanism. Fallback if a new chapter is refused: Ch. 76 §76.4.4 + Ch. 58 §58.4.4 + the J.5 table. Editor's note: the health-signals/telemetry chapter proposed in another batch targets the same slot — one combined 'Production Telemetry' chapter would limit renumbering.
- **What is missing / scope notes:** Covered is the *what* (decompose per stage, compare to venue clocks); missing is the *how* across processes and the venue field reference. ## G24 — Rust for the hot path: ownership vs zero-allocation patterns, unsafe at the hardware boundary, atomics/memory model parity, bounds-check elision, and codegen parity with C++ measured on the book's own kernels

- **Theme:** C. Languages & interop · **Proposed kind:** new-chapter · **Sources:** languages#1

- **Why this priority:** Explicitly author-requested area (implementation-languages): the only Rust content is a 389-word prose appendix with no code, asm or measurement, and five chapters forward-reference Rust material that does not exist.
- **Refined placement:** One chapter-length treatment, not a new Part on its own. Preferred: a single new chapter 'Rust for the Hot Path: Ownership, `unsafe` and Codegen Parity' superseding §B.1 (which becomes a pointer), written to the book's conventions and measured on the book's own kernels (Ch. 25 book update, Ch. 34 SPSC ring, Ch. 53 decoder, Ch. 29 SIMD loop): ownership vs the Ch. 23–24 zero-allocation patterns (arena crates, `GlobalAlloc`/`Allocator` API), `#[repr(C)]`/`#[repr(align(64))]`/`CachePadded` vs Ch. 9/33, `std::sync::atomic` + `Ordering` parity with Ch. 30 (same asm on x86 and ARM — Appendix A), `&mut` as `restrict` (Ch. 21) and what the vectorizer gains, bounds-check elision (iterators vs indexing, `get_unchecked`), `unsafe` at the hardware boundary (wire overlay, `mmap`, NIC rings), `const fn`/traits vs `dyn`/`Result`/`async` so the five dangling cross-references resolve, FFI with the C++ core (fold §B.5). Placement: if the companion inline-asm and other-language chapters from the languages batch are adopted, group them as a short Part between Part XI and Part XII (capstone stays last, one scripted renumber); if Rust is adopted alone, the smaller footprint is the final chapter of Part III after Ch. 22 (prerequisites Ch. 4, 19–21) or expanding Appendix B in place to chapter depth — avoid renumbering 71–76 for one chapter.
- **What is missing / scope notes:** Everything measurable is missing, and the existing survey already makes claims the book's rules forbid without evidence ('zero runtime cost', 'zero-cost abstractions'). The chapter must show Godbolt diffs (bounds checks elided or not; `Ordering` → identical `mov`/`xchg`/`lock cmpxchg`; `&mut` aliasing enabling the same vectorization as `__restrict__`) and side-by-side benchmark tables for the chosen kernels with rustc/LLVM versions named, plus an honest list of where parity breaks (panic paths and unwinding tables, `Vec` growth, overflow checks, no `restrict`-like control on raw pointers). Fix the five dangling cross-references in any case.

## G25 — Hand-written and inline assembly: GCC/Clang extended-asm anatomy (constraints, clobbers, volatile, asm goto), intrinsics-first, when the compiler cannot be beaten (rdpmc, doorbells, TSX, fences), and proving it with measurement

- **Theme:** C. Languages & interop · **Proposed kind:** new-chapter · **Sources:** languages#2
- **Verdict:** partially-covered · **Priority:** high
- **Why this priority:** Explicitly requested implementation-languages area, and the asm-statement mechanics behind tools the book already leans on (DoNotOptimize/ClobberMemory, compiler barriers, rdtsc fencing, doorbell ordering, user-space PMU reads) are nowhere taught.
- **Refined placement:** New compact chapter at the end of Part III after Ch. 22 (Build Toolchain) - 'Inline Assembly & Intrinsics: When the Compiler Can't Be Beaten' - the writing-side sequel to Ch. 4, with the book's standard measure-it / verify-the-codegen sections. Not a section in Ch. 4 (Part I is methodology; the material needs its own measurement and codegen proof). Minimal-footprint fallback if no new chapter: a §4.4.4 'Writing it back: extended asm, constraints and clobbers' in Ch. 4 plus a §2.4.4 'User-space counter reads with rdpmc' in Ch. 2, and a one-line reveal in Ch. 3 §3.4.1 that DoNotOptimize/ClobberMemory are extended-asm statements.
- **What is missing / scope notes:** Drop TSX/`xbegin` (deprecated and microcode-disabled on Ice Lake-SP-era Intel parts, i.e. the book's reference machine) in favour of: GCC/Clang extended-asm anatomy (output/input operands, constraint letters, `+r`/`=&r` early-clobber, `memory`/`cc` clobbers, `volatile`, `asm goto`, `asm inline`) and what the optimizer may and may not do around an asm statement (no scheduling across it, no CSE/hoisting of volatile asm); reveal that Ch. 3's `DoNotOptimize`/`ClobberMemory` and Ch. 30's compiler barrier are extended asm; the intrinsics-first rule with the book's own uses (Ch. 10/17/28/29/32) as evidence that the compiler still allocates registers and schedules around intrinsics; the irreducible cases - `rdpmc` user-space PMU reads for cycle-exact benchmarking (ties Ch. 2/3/Appendix L), `cpuid`/`serialize`/`lfence` ordering (Ch. 17), `clflushopt`/`clwb`, write-combining doorbell stores (Ch. 66); prove each with a measurement and a Godbolt diff against the intrinsic version, and show a mis-declared clobber silently corrupting a register.
- **Closest existing coverage:**
  - 17-timekeeping-tsc-rdtsc-clocks.md §17.2.1 and §17.3 lines 74-133 (intrinsic rdtsc/rdtscp/lfence, fenced vs unfenced measured)
  - 29-simd-vectorization.md §29.4.2 'Intrinsics when the compiler won't' lines 112-118
  - 32-spinlocks-backoff-contention-control.md §32.2 line 52 and code line 74 (_mm_pause; tpause/umwait in prose)
  - 10-software-prefetching-nontemporal-stores.md §10.2.1/§10.5 (__builtin_prefetch, __builtin_nontemporal_store, _mm_sfence emission verified)
  - 30-cpp-memory-model-atomics.md §30.4.2 line 129 (asm volatile memory clobber named as compiler-only barrier - a warning, not a technique)
  - 04-reading-the-machine-asm.md §4.2.1 lines 33-41 (SysV register/ABI table - reading only)
  - 66-pcie-host-device-boundary.md §66.4.1 lines 97-100 and 127 (doorbell/sfence ordering in prose)
- **Evidence (greps and reads):** Greps across /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/*.md: `inline asm|inline assembly|__asm__|asm volatile|asm goto|extended asm|asm\s*\(|clobber|naked|hand-written assembly|\.S file|nasm` -> one real hit, 30-cpp-memory-model-atomics.md line 129 (`asm volatile("":::"memory")` named in a don't-do-this bullet as a compiler-only barrier); every other 'clobber' hit is Ch. 3/Appendix L's `ClobberMemory` or Ch. 4:39 'caller-saved (free to clobber)'. `rdpmc|xbegin|TSX|RTM|HLE|mwait|umwait` -> only Ch. 32 lines 52/145/195 naming `tpause`/`umwait`/`umonitor` in prose. No code fence in any chapter contains an asm statement; the only `#include <x86intrin.h>`/`<immintrin.h>` are Ch. 17:74, 32:74, 37:64, 42:73, 46:57. What does exist is intrinsics-first in practice, with measurement: Ch. 17 lines 74-80 (`__rdtsc`/`__rdtscp`/`_mm_lfence`; fenced vs unfenced cost measured at line 133), Ch. 32 line 74 (`_mm_pause`), Ch. 10 line 221 (`__builtin_nontemporal_store` + `_mm_sfence`, codegen checked in §10.5), Ch. 29 §29.4.2 lines 112-118 ('Intrinsics when the compiler won't' - the book's only explicit when-to-drop-down rule), Ch. 66 lines 99-100/127 (descriptor-then-doorbell `sfence` ordering, prose), Appendix A line 53 (ARM `__yield()`/`__wfe()` intrinsics). Ch. 4 (§4.2.1-§4.4.3) teaches reading asm and the SysV ABI, never writing it; Ch. 2 line 40 says `perf_event_open` is 'almost never' called directly, so user-space `rdpmc` counter reads have no home. Ch. 3 §3.4.1 describes `DoNotOptimize`/`ClobberMemory` as 'a compiler barrier' without revealing they are extended-asm statements.

## G32 — The sequencer architecture: total-order broadcast, replicated deterministic consumers, and hot/warm standby across hosts (journal shipping, state-machine replication, RPO/RTO)

- **Theme:** D. Design patterns & architecture · **Proposed kind:** new-chapter · **Sources:** design-patterns#1, hft-domain#4, resilience-ha#1
- **Verdict:** genuinely-missing · **Priority:** high
- **Why this priority:** Ch. 74-76's deterministic-core, replay and CI-gate story presupposes a totally ordered input stream and bit-identical replicas that the book never shows how to produce, fan out or fail over to - the architectural keystone of a real trading system, in the requested design-patterns area.
- **Refined placement:** One new chapter in Part XII between Ch. 75 (Capture) and Ch. 76 (Case Study) - 'The Sequencer: Total-Order Broadcast, Replicated Deterministic Consumers & Hot/Warm Standby' - with prerequisites Ch. 34/37 (rings), 50/51/65 (shm rings, multicast, Aeron), 54 (gap recovery), 58 (hardware timestamps), 74 and 75; Ch. 76 stays last and gains the sequencer in its architecture diagram. Insert a cross-reference at Ch. 74 §74.2.2 line 57 ('a single ordered log' - produced by the sequencer, Ch. N) and update Appendix J.6 line 72 and Appendix F. Not a new mini-Part; scope the chapter to sequencer + replication + standby and leave leases, fencing tokens and split-brain to the separate failover item (resilience-ha#2).
- **What is missing / scope notes:** Cover: the sequencer (a process, a switch or an FPGA - ties Ch. 60/69) stamping a global sequence on market data after A/B arbitration (Ch. 54), order acks/fills, timer fires, operator commands and Ch. 73 config changes, fanned out over multicast (Ch. 51) or shm rings (Ch. 50/37) - contrasted with Ch. 74's point-to-point ring topology; the added hop measured with hardware timestamps (Ch. 58) as the price of one truth; replicated deterministic consumers (gateway, risk, strategy, journal, GUI) folding the same stream through a Ch. 74 state machine to bit-identical state, verified by state-hash comparison; hot standby with output suppression / single-emitter election vs warm standby + journal shipping vs cold snapshot restore, each with RPO/RTO and the failures it survives (process, host, rack, site) and failover time measured replica-vs-restart against §74.3; snapshot + replay catch-up (ties Ch. 75 §75.4.4); Aeron Cluster/Raft as the off-the-shelf comparison (Ch. 65 line 64); pitfalls - the sequencer as single point of failure and sequencer failover, consumers that read a clock, two emitters.
- **Closest existing coverage:**
  - appendix-J-market-structure-primer.md J.6 line 72 (one-line glossary entry)
  - 65-transport-beyond-tcp.md §65.4.1 line 64 (Aeron Cluster Raft replicated state machines - one sentence)
  - 74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.2.2 line 57 (assumes a single ordered input log) and §74.3 lines 75-94 (single-host restart only, no cross-host replica)
- **Evidence (greps and reads):** Greps across chapters/*.md: `sequencer` -> appendix-J-market-structure-primer.md line 72 only ('Sequencer - in some designs, a single process that stamps a total order on events for deterministic replay (Ch. 74, 75)'). `total.order|totally.ordered|global order|atomic broadcast` -> Ch. 30 lines 28/42/119/168/191 (seq_cst memory ordering), Ch. 65 lines 35/96 (transport stream ordering), Appendix J - none architectural. `replicated state machine|state.machine replication|\bRaft\b|Paxos|Aeron Cluster` -> 65-transport-beyond-tcp.md line 64 only ('Aeron Cluster provides Raft-based fault-tolerant replicated state machines (Ch. 74's deterministic state machine, replicated) for consensus/failover' - one sentence, plus reference lines 124/132). `\bstandby\b|hot spare|\breplicas?\b|replication|active.passive|active.active|journal shipping|log shipping|lockstep|\bRPO\b|\bRTO\b|split.brain|leader election|promotion|failover` -> only unrelated senses: Ch. 16:145 per-NUMA-node copies of read-mostly data, Ch. 60 §60.4.3 layer-1 signal replication for capture/fan-out, Ch. 70:96 'intra-datacenter state replication' clause, lockstep in Ch. 8/32/67 (parallel arrays, backoff, GPU warps), Appendix K:57 (redundant gateways 'give order entry a failover path' - one bullet), Ch. 76:90/124 ('the failover (Ch. 74)' as a simulated tail condition). 74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.2.2 line 57 assumes 'a single ordered log (Ch. 75)' of every input without saying who produces the order or how gateway/risk/strategy/journal agree on it; §74.3 lines 75-94 ('failover/restart time') measures only single-host crash-only detect -> restart -> recover -> warm, with no replica or promotion; §74.4.1 lines 98-106 is point-to-point shm rings; 37-the-disruptor-pattern.md §37.4.1 lines 130-133 is in-process one-ring fan-out; ## G33 — Idempotent order entry and order-state recovery: correlation tokens (slot+generation ClOrdIDs), the preallocated in-flight order table, duplicate detection, the unknown-state matrix after a crash, reconciliation against drop-copy and venue order status, position rebuild

- **Theme:** D. Design patterns & architecture · **Proposed kind:** new-chapter · **Sources:** design-patterns#2, resilience-ha#4, health-app#4
- **Verdict:** genuinely-missing · **Priority:** high
- **Why this priority:** A duplicated or orphaned order is a real position and real money, and nothing in the 76 chapters develops order identity, in-flight state, or post-crash reconciliation — a central operating concern of a tick-to-trade system and squarely in the author-requested design-patterns area.
- **Refined placement:** New chapter in Part XII, placed after Ch. 74 (and after the pre-trade risk-gate chapter G38 if adopted, since the gate precedes the gateway on the order path) and before Ch. 75 Capture; renumber 75-76. Rationale: Ch. 74 §74.2.1 already assigns working-order state to the gateway and §74.4.4's kill-switch must know the working set it cancels; Ch. 75's journal is what makes ClOrdID allocation crash-safe (journal-before-send). Depends on the Ch. 25 slot-map section (G39) for the preallocated in-flight table and on the timers chapter (G36) for ack timeouts. Fix the dangling cross-refs when written: App F 'Drop-copy (Ch. 74)', App K.6 'reconnect and resynchronize (J.3)', Ch. 73 §73.4.4 'retiring a strategy that has open orders'.
- **What is missing / scope notes:** Order identity as slot-index+generation integers (Ch. 28 packing), never strings on the hot path; the fixed-capacity in-flight order table (Ch. 24 pool / G39 slot map) with O(1) ack/fill/reject correlation and generation checks rejecting late or duplicate responses; the per-order state machine from App J.3 as data with sent/acked/last-fill timestamps and unacked-order aging (G36 timers); the unknown-state matrix after a crash {never sent, sent-not-acked, acked, partial, filled, cancelled, replaced} and the resend-vs-don't-resend hazard per order type (IOC vs resting); venue duplicate-ClOrdID rejection as backstop; session resync (FIX MsgSeqNum resend/gap-fill, OUCH/SoupBinTCP sequence) on reconnect; reconciliation of working orders and positions against drop-copy and venue order-status requests, and position rebuild from fills+journal; measure: correlation cost, time-to-known-state after a kill. Keep position accumulators themselves in the G38 risk chapter; this chapter owns post-send state and recovery.
- **Closest existing coverage:**
  - 74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.2.1 (order-gateway role bullet: owns sequence numbers and working-order state — one sentence)
  - 74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.4.4 (gateway mass-cancels a dead strategy's working orders — mechanism only)
  - appendix-J-market-structure-primer.md J.3 (order lifecycle New->Ack/Reject->fills->Cancel/Replace->terminal, prose)
  - appendix-F-glossary.md L12 Drop-copy definition (points to Ch. 74, which has no such content)
- **Evidence (greps and reads):** Greps run across chapters/*.md (grep -n -i -E): 'idempoten' -> 65-transport-beyond-tcp.md L96/L120 only, both pointing at Ch. 53/54 market-data dedup; 'clordid|client order id|client-order|order token|in-flight order|duplicate order|possdup|order status|position rebuild|unknown order|unknown state|mass cancel' -> 0 hits (Ch. 73 §73.4.4 L96 has 'in-flight work' in the drain-cutover sense only; Appendix J.5 lists OUCH message names but no token/ClOrdID field); 'reconcil' -> 08 L219 (exercise wording) and 50 L171/L181 (shm segment 'reconcile') only; 'drop.copy' -> 38 L123, 52 L66, 57 L67, 72 L13 (named as a connection type in passing) + appendix-F-glossary.md L12 defines Drop-copy '(Ch. 74)' while 74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md never contains the word (dangling pointer); 'cancel.on.disconnect|working order' -> 74 §74.2.1 L37, §74.4.4 L127, checklist L169, App F L17/L23 only; 'resend|sequence reset|logon|MsgSeqNum|session recovery|reconnect' -> only market-data retransmission (54 §54.4.2, 65 L87) and appendix-K L57 'sessions are designed to reconnect and resynchronize (J.3) without losing order state' — J.3 (read in full, L31-42) is a prose order-lifecycle list with no resync, ID, or recovery content. Read: Ch. 74 §74.2.1-74.2.2 and §74.4.1-74.4.5 in full — the gateway 'owns ... sequence numbers, working-order state' and 'can cancel-on-disconnect / mass-cancel the dead component's orders' are single sentences; no in-flight table, correlation, duplicate detection, unknown-state analysis, drop-copy reconciliation or position rebuild anywhere.

## G34 — Flow control on the hot path: backpressure policies for a full channel (block/drop-newest/drop-oldest/conflate/sample), conflation and latest-value fan-out to slow consumers, staleness budgets, degraded modes and load shedding at the open

- **Theme:** D. Design patterns & architecture · **Proposed kind:** new-chapter · **Sources:** design-patterns#3, hft-domain#10, resilience-ha#7
- **Verdict:** partially-covered · **Priority:** high
- **Why this priority:** Every trading system has a consumer slower than the market-open burst; five chapters each state an ad-hoc full-buffer policy and none owns the taxonomy, conflation, or staleness measurement, and trading on a stale view is a direct P&L hazard — central and author-requested (design-patterns).
- **Refined placement:** New chapter in Part VI, immediately after Ch. 37 (or after the threading-topologies chapter G35 if both are adopted: Ch. 37 -> Topologies -> Flow Control -> Ch. 38). It generalizes Ch. 34's 'push fails when full' and Ch. 37's gating/batching, and its conflation structure is Ch. 35's seqlock per instrument plus a Ch. 28 bitmap. Once it exists, Ch. 34 §34.4.1, Ch. 37 §37.4.1, Ch. 54 §54.5, Ch. 71 §71.6 and Ch. 75 §75.5 cross-reference it instead of each restating a policy; Ch. 74 §74.4.4 and Ch. 53 §53.2.4 point at its degraded-mode section. Fallback if no new chapter is wanted: Ch. 35 §35.4.3 'Conflated / latest-value publication' + Ch. 34 §34.4.4 'Overflow policies for a full ring' + Ch. 74 §74.4.6 'Degraded modes at the open' — but that re-scatters exactly what the analysts flagged.
- **What is missing / scope notes:** Missing and to be written: the policy taxonomy for a full channel (block/throttle producer — never on the tick-to-trade path; drop-newest; drop-oldest/overwrite; conflate per key; sample/degrade) mapped to consumer class (strategy, risk, gateway, journal, GUI/monitoring); conflation structures — per-instrument latest-value slot (seqlock) + dirty bitmap drained with tzcnt (Ch. 28) + deduplicating dirty queue, last-write-wins vs merged semantics (summed volume, trade prints must not conflate); depth-1 mailbox/triple-buffer for single-reader latest-value fan-out; staleness as a first-class metric (sequence lag per channel, PTP message age — Ch. 58) with a staleness budget per consumer; degraded modes and load shedding at the open (what to drop/conflate/pull, who owns the mode state machine — Ch. 74 supervisor/risk); measure the latency each policy preserves under a replayed market-open burst (Ch. 75). Existing per-chapter statements become one-line pointers.
- **Closest existing coverage:**
  - 34-lock-free-data-structures.md §34.4.1 and §34.5 (bounded ring: push fails when full; policy left to caller)
  - 37-the-disruptor-pattern.md §37.4.1 (gating on slowest consumer as backpressure) and §37.4.2 (batching self-corrects when a consumer falls behind)
  - 71-zero-overhead-logging.md §71.3 and §71.6 (logger ring-full policy: drop-and-count vs overwrite-oldest, never block)
  - 75-capture-persistence-replay-storage.md §75.5 (capture: drop-and-flag or shed at capture layer, never stall the hot path)
  - 54-reliable-multicast-feed-recovery.md §54.4.3 and §54.5 (per-instrument stale/pause policy during gap recovery; slow consumer manufactures gaps)
  - 35-seqlocks-single-writer-publication.md §35.1-35.2 (seqlock latest-snapshot publication — the conflation building block)
  - 01-the-latency-mindset.md §1.2.1 (queueing-theory motivation for headroom)
- **Evidence (greps and reads):** Greps run: 'back.?pressure' -> 34-lock-free-data-structures.md §34.4.1 L123 and §34.5 L154 ('natural backpressure (push fails when full -> caller handles it)', 'handle fullness explicitly' — policy left to reader); 37-the-disruptor-pattern.md §37.2 L35, §37.4.1 L136, checklist L175 (wrap protection gating the producer on the slowest consumer = 'natural backpressure: a slow stage throttles the producer'); 54 §54.5 L100 ('never let downstream slowness back-pressure the receive path'); 71 §71.3 L62, §71.6 L119, ex.4 L132 ('drop-and-count vs overwrite-oldest, never block the producer' — logger only); 75 §75.3 L54, §75.5 L71/L116, checklist L138 ('drop-and-flag or shed at the capture layer, never stall the trade' — capture only). 'conflat' -> 11 L165, 23 L17/L26 only, all the verb 'conflates'. 'latest.value|last.value' -> 63 L85 (RDMA WRITE 'latest value' phrase). 'dirty bitmap|dirty.bit|load.shedding|degraded mode|triple.buffer|mailbox|overflow polic|drop.oldest|drop.newest' -> 0 ('shed' once in 75 L116; 'degraded' only 58 L94 about PTP). 'slow consumer|slow reader' -> 54 L100 only (as a cause of self-inflicted gaps). 'stale' -> 54 §54.4.3 L83-92 (per-instrument stale flag + pause/continue policy during gap recovery) and 53 L169. 'falling behind|fall behind' -> 37 §37.1 L17 and §37.4.2 L140-150 (batch size grows when behind — catch-up, read in full), 53 §53.2.4 L54-60 and §53.6 L225 (RX ring overflow — hardware/driver side only). Read Ch. 35 §35.1-35.2 (seqlock snapshot of BBO for many readers — the building block, no conflation) and Ch. 1 §1.2.1 L45-50 (queueing: run under-utilized — motivation only).

## G35 — Threading topologies and the hot loop: run-to-completion vs staged pipelines vs shard fan-out, polling many sources in one loop, hop-cost model, and zero-copy handoff ownership (the slot lifecycle across stages and processes)

- **Theme:** D. Design patterns & architecture · **Proposed kind:** new-chapter · **Sources:** design-patterns#4, design-patterns#10
- **Verdict:** partially-covered · **Priority:** high
- **Why this priority:** How many threads touch a message and in what order the loop polls its inputs fixes the latency floor before any micro-optimization; the book asserts the answer in four places and measures the hop cost twice but never develops the decision or the loop — central and in the author-requested design-patterns area.
- **Refined placement:** New chapter in Part VI immediately after Ch. 37 (before the flow-control chapter G34 and Ch. 38): it generalizes §37.4.1's graphs and §31.4.1's bullets, and Ch. 39 §39.4.1 (pinned-core scheduler), Ch. 41 §41.4.2, Ch. 62 §62.4.3 and Ch. 74 §74.2.1 can then point at it for the topology decision. Own the zero-copy slot-ownership lifecycle as a section of this chapter (it spans stages and processes), with one-paragraph pointers from Ch. 37 §37.2, Ch. 50 §50.4.2 and Ch. 49 §49.5; if the chapter is not adopted, the slot-ownership piece alone fits as Ch. 37 §37.4.3 and the topology decision as Ch. 31 §31.4.3, but the hot-loop structure then has no natural home.
- **What is missing / scope notes:** To write: the three topologies with a cost model — run-to-completion (zero hops), staged pipeline (each hop = cross-core line transfer + possible wakeup, using Appendix E / Ch. 39 §39.3 / Ch. 57 §57.3 numbers), shard fan-out by symbol — and the rule for when a split pays (per-stage work exceeds hop cost; what it does to p99.9 vs throughput); the hot loop that polls many sources in one iteration (feed A/B, order-ack channel, control/config ring, timer queue as a polled input — G36) with polling order, per-source batch caps so the feed cannot starve the ack channel, and the measured per-iteration overhead; mapping stages/shards onto isolated cores and NUMA nodes (Ch. 16/42); the slot lifecycle as an explicit state machine free -> claimed -> published -> consumed-by-all-gated-stages -> free, which sequence transition grants/revokes each right, std::span views bounded by the last stage's barrier, move-only [[nodiscard]] slot handles, and the same protocol across processes (Ch. 50) — with a test that a stage holding a view one sequence too long is detected.
- **Closest existing coverage:**
  - 31-multithreading-concurrency-foundations.md §31.4.1 (shard-by-symbol and pipeline-of-single-threaded-stages as two bullets; checklist L152/L156)
  - 37-the-disruptor-pattern.md §37.2 (claim/write/release-publish mechanics; slot reuse) and §37.4.1 (pipeline / fan-out / diamond dependency graphs; trading pipeline example)
  - 39-std-execution-senders-receivers.md §39.3 and ex. 3 (measured cost of a scheduler hop; keep the hot pipeline on one scheduler)
  - 57-flow-steering-receive-scaling.md §57.3 (cross-core handoff cost: HITM + wrong-L3 + wakeup, measured)
  - 62-kernel-bypass-userspace-networking.md §62.4.3 (single-threaded run-to-completion loop asserted; heavier work off-core)
  - 41-context-switching-mitigation.md §41.4.1-41.4.2 (one hot thread per core; busy-poll vs block)
  - 47-linux-native-io.md §47.4.1 (one epoll loop per thread, sharded)
  - 49-zero-copy-modern-kernel-fast-path.md §49.5 and 48-io-uring-deep-dive.md §48.5 (buffer lifetime until completion — kernel side only)
  - 50-inter-process-communication.md §50.4.2 (offsets/indices, single-writer per ring across processes)

## G36 — Timers and deadlines without jitter: hierarchical timing wheels vs heaps, deadline queues polled from the busy loop with rdtsc, timer fires as replayable inputs; and for sleeping threads: hrtimers, timer slack, clock_nanosleep/TIMER_ABSTIME, timerfd and wake-up latency

- **Theme:** D. Design patterns & architecture · **Proposed kind:** new-chapter · **Sources:** design-patterns#5, hft-domain#9, algorithms-numerics#5, os-kernel-depth#1
- **Verdict:** genuinely-missing · **Priority:** high
- **Why this priority:** Every order, quote, heartbeat and recovery request carries a deadline and a busy-poll engine must expire thousands per iteration without a syscall, yet the book has zero timer-management content and never explains the kernel timer stack behind the cyclictest numbers it relies on — central, author-requested (design-patterns), and flagged independently by four analysts.
- **Refined placement:** One new chapter in Part VII, immediately after Ch. 41 (Context Switching) and before Ch. 42: Ch. 41 §41.4.2's busy-poll-vs-block is exactly the fork between the two halves. Half A (the busy-poll core): a user-space deadline queue polled once per loop iteration with rdtsc (Ch. 17) — hierarchical timing wheel vs 4-ary heap vs sorted intrusive list (Ch. 25), O(1) cancel for TTL-style timers, pooled timer nodes (Ch. 24), bounded per-tick work, timer fires emitted as replayable input events into the Ch. 74 core. Half B (sleeping/housekeeping threads): the Linux timer stack — hrtimers vs the kernel timer wheel, CONFIG_HZ and tick-aligned expiry, timer slack (PR_SET_TIMERSLACK, /proc/<pid>/timerslack_ns, zero under SCHED_FIFO/RR/DEADLINE), drift-free periodic loops with clock_nanosleep(TIMER_ABSTIME), timerfd in epoll/io_uring loops, and measured wake-up latency — which also explains what the cyclictest numbers in Ch. 6 §6.3 and Ch. 45 §45.3 actually measure. Cross-reference from Ch. 45 §45.3, Ch. 53 §53.4.3 (reorder-window timeout), Ch. 74 §74.4.3 (heartbeat timeouts), Ch. 46 §46.4.1 (warming cadence); consumed by G33 (ack timeouts) and G38 (throttle windows). Splitting the halves across Part VI and Part VII is acceptable but one chapter is the smaller footprint.
- **What is missing / scope notes:** Measure it: per-iteration cost of checking the deadline queue with 10^4-10^6 armed timers (wheel vs heap), expiry-burst worst case, and the wake-up-latency distribution of nanosleep / timerfd / futex-timed-wait under default vs zero timer slack and under SCHED_OTHER vs SCHED_FIFO on an isolated core. Pitfalls: reading the clock inside the deterministic core, std::this_thread::sleep_for on a hot thread, epoll_wait with a non-zero timeout on the hot path, unbounded wheel cascading, timer-ID reuse (ties G39 generational handles).
- **Evidence (greps and reads):** Greps run across chapters/*.md: 'timing wheel|timer wheel|time wheel|hierarchical tim|deadline queue|timer queue|timer heap' -> 0; 'timerfd|hrtimer|high.resolution timer|timer slack|timer_slack|timerslack|PR_SET|clock_nanosleep|TIMER_ABSTIME|jiffies' -> 0; 'CONFIG_HZ|tick rate' -> 45 L21/L110/L132 only (the scheduler tick as a jitter source); 'nanosleep' -> 17 L90 (a 200 ms calibration delay in example code); 'sleep_for|sleep_until' -> 03 L53/L74 (pacing a benchmark), 35 L107 and 36 L97 (test-harness sleeps); 'priority_queue|min.heap|binary heap|std::make_heap' -> 0; 'wake.?up latency|wakeup' -> 06 §6.3 L62-72 and 45 §45.3 L50-62 (cyclictest 'sleeps a thread for a fixed interval, then measures how late the wake-up was' — read in full: measurement only, no hrtimer/slack mechanism), 06 ex.5 L153 (C-state wake tail), 42 §42.3 L60, 34/50 lost wakeups; 'timeout' -> 53 §53.2.4 L57 and §53.4.3 L170/§53.6 L227 (reorder-window timeout — policy, no mechanism for checking it), 74 §74.3 L81/§74.4.3 L119/ex.5 L159 (heartbeat timeout tuning), 47 L159 (zero-timeout epoll_wait); 'deadline' -> 01 L19 (metaphor), 45 L44 (SCHED_DEADLINE one bullet), 59 §59.4.1/§59.5 (TX departure-time deadlines in the qdisc — not application timers); 'TTL|IOC|FOK|GTD|expir|time.in.force' -> appendix-J L36 TIF definitions and 09 L88 a `tif` bit-field; 'cancel.if.no.ack|ack timeout|unack' -> only TCP unacked-data (51 L21/L24, 52 L30/L32); 'periodic' -> heartbeats/snapshots named as periodic (74, 75, 71 L86) with no timer facility. Read Ch. 74 §74.2.2 L41-57: 'time is an INPUT ... each timer fire is a record in the ordered log' — the determinism requirement with no implementation.

## G38 — The pre-trade risk gate and kill-switch path in software: 15c3-5/RTS 6 check inventory, data layout for a ~40 ns gate, position accumulators and checked notional math, throttles, automated kill-switch triggers and safe-state

- **Refined placement:** New chapter in Part XII immediately after Ch. 74 and before Ch. 75 (and before the order-entry/recovery chapter G33 if both are adopted, since the gate precedes the gateway on the order path); renumber 75-76. Ch. 74 §74.2.1's Risk bullet and §74.4.4's trigger parenthetical point at it; Ch. 76 §76.3's ~40 ns row, Ch. 69 §69.4.1 (fabric version) and App K.4's throttle sentence (currently 'Ch. 76') are re-pointed. Builds on Ch. 25 (flat per-symbol arrays), Ch. 8/9 (hot/cold split, cache-line sizing), Ch. 27/72 (scaled integers, checked math), Ch. 35/73 (limit publication and hot reload), G36 (throttle windows), G37 (Price/Notional types). The post-send reconciliation belongs to G33; position accumulators updated from fills live here.

## G39 — Slot maps, generational handles and relocatable object graphs (indices, not pointers) for order tables, books and timer queues

- **Theme:** D. Design patterns & architecture · **Proposed kind:** new-section-in-existing-chapter · **Sources:** design-patterns#8
- **Verdict:** partially-covered · **Priority:** medium

- **Refined placement:** Ch. 25 — new §25.4.4 'Slot maps and generational handles' inserted before the LOB case study (which becomes §25.4.5 and adopts the slot map for its orderID -> Order table), with a §25.3 benchmark row (slot-map lookup vs flat hash vs raw pointer, generation-check cost) and §25.5 pitfalls (generation wrap, reusing a handle across processes without the generation, skipping the check on trusted paths). One-line forward references from Ch. 24 §24.4.2 (pool free-list), Ch. 34 §34.4.3, Ch. 50 §50.4.2, Ch. 72 §72.4.2; optional Appendix H row for std::inplace_vector (C++26). Write it before G33 (in-flight order table) and G36 (timer nodes), which should adopt it.
- **What is missing / scope notes:** Content: dense value array + sparse indirection table + free-list of slot indices + per-slot generation; Handle{uint32 index, uint32 generation} packed into 64 bits (Ch. 28); O(1) insert/erase/lookup; erase-by-swap keeping values contiguous for iteration (Ch. 8) vs stable-slot variant for intrusive links; validation on dereference as one predictable compare (Ch. 13); relative/offset handles for shared memory (Ch. 50); debug-mode poisoning; codegen check that the handle unpack is two shifts and a compare.
- **Closest existing coverage:**
  - 25-hotpath-hostile-stl-cache-friendly-containers.md §25.4.3 'Index handles over pointers' bullet (names slot maps / generational indices); §25.4.1 and §25.5 (use indices for stable identity in flat containers); §25.4.4 LOB case study (orderID->Order via flat hash map — not yet a slot map)

  - 72-secure-programming-low-latency.md §72.4.2 (index + generation validated on deref as the cheap use-after-free defense)
  - 50-inter-process-communication.md §50.4.2 and §50.5 (offsets/indices never pointers across address spaces; versioned fixed layout)
  - 36-safe-memory-reclamation.md §36.5 (tagged pointers/indices solve ABA together with reclamation)
- **Evidence (greps and reads):** Greps run: 'slot.?map' -> 25-hotpath-hostile-stl-cache-friendly-containers.md §25.4.3 L138 only ('Index handles over pointers ... a common low-latency idiom (slot maps, generational indices)' — one bullet, read L130-142); 'generational|generation (counter|check|number|tag)' -> 72-secure-programming-low-latency.md §72.4.2 L89 ('generation counters / tagged handles instead of raw pointers (an index + generation, validated on deref — catches stale handles cheaply)') and checklist L172 — a bullet, no structure; 'tagged pointer' -> 34 §34.4.3 L135-142 (version/tag in high pointer bits or DWCAS; 'use a fixed pool indexed by integer ... index-based, allocation-free structures that don't have ABA'), 36 L158/L182, App F L65; 'handle.based|index handle|erase.by.swap|swap.and.pop|swap.remove|free.list index|stable (id|handle|index)|inplace_vector' -> 0 beyond the Ch. 25 L138 bullet; 'offset_ptr|relative pointer|self.relative' -> 0 literal, but 50 §50.4.2 L145 and §50.5 L154 develop 'offsets/indices, never pointers' as the cardinal shared-memory rule (base+offset, versioned fixed layout — read L138-162) and 74 §74.4.1 repeats it. Read Ch. 25 §25.4.1 L121 ('use indices or an intrusive design if you need stable identity'), §25.4.4 L144-165 (LOB case study keeps 'an orderID -> Order*/index map, itself a flat hash map' — the slot map is not adopted) and §25.5 ('Use indices/handles (§25.4.3)'). Verdict: the idiom and all of its rationales (relocatability, ABA-freedom, stale-handle detection, cross-address-space validity) are each stated in a bullet, but no chapter shows the structure, its operations, its cost, or a benchmark.

## G41 — Concepts, constrained overload sets and compile-time dispatch: tag dispatch vs if constexpr vs SFINAE vs concepts on one feed-handler example, with identical codegen proven and build-time/diagnostic cost compared

- **Theme:** E. Modern C++ depth · **Proposed kind:** new-section-in-existing-chapter · **Sources:** cpp-features#1

## G42 — Compile-time protocol decoders: from SBE/ITCH schema to code with class-type NTTPs and fixed_string, consteval parsing of field lists, generated overlay accessors verified in asm, and C++26 static reflection

- **Theme:** E. Modern C++ depth · **Proposed kind:** new-chapter · **Sources:** cpp-features#2
- **Verdict:** genuinely-missing · **Priority:** high
- **Why this priority:** The decode path is the hottest loop in the system, Ch. 18 already promises this chapter's content, and it is the one place Part III's compile-time philosophy meets the tick-to-trade path — currently absent and author-requested.
- **Refined placement:** New chapter in Part III after Ch. 19 (before Ch. 20): 'Compile-Time Protocol Decoders: From Schema to Code'. Build it on shipping C++20 — class-type NTTPs (P0732) + a `fixed_string`, `consteval` parsing of an SBE/ITCH field list into offsets/sizes/byteswap flags, accessor generation with templates + `if constexpr`, `static_assert` against `offsetof`/sizeof and the schema version; §N.3 measures decode ns equal to Ch. 53's hand-written decoder plus the build-time cost; §N.5 shows asm identical to Ch. 53 §53.5. Treat C++26 static reflection (P2996, `^^`, `members_of`) as one clearly labelled forward-looking section (experimental Clang only, not GCC) — the chapter must stand without it. Add Appendix H rows for class-type NTTPs and reflection. This renumbers Ch. 20-76 (the scripted renumber tool exists; pair with G43's chapter if adopted). Fallback without renumbering: §18.4.3 'From schema to field table' (NTTP/fixed_string/consteval) + §53.4.5 'Generating the decoder at compile time' (accessors + asm) + the App H rows.
- **What is missing / scope notes:** Entirely missing. Keep the C++20 path primary; state toolchain support (GCC 10+/Clang 12+ for class-type NTTPs) and gate reflection behind feature-test macros per Appendix H conventions. Cross-reference Ch. 71 (binary log record layouts are the same problem) and Ch. 75 (schema/version archived with captures).
- **Closest existing coverage:**
  - 18-compile-time-mechanics.md §18.1 (line 15) — promise only; §18.4.2 (lines 139-165) constexpr table generation (digit pairs, tick sizes) — adjacent technique, not schema-driven
  - 53-zero-copy-wire-market-data-decoding.md §53.2.2 (line 41) — 'use the external SBE generator'; §53.5 (lines 185-196) — verified mov+bswap lowering of hand-written offsets
- **Evidence (greps and reads):** grep -n -i -E 'reflection|reflexpr|P2996|\^\^|members_of|define_aggregate' chapters/*.md → 1 hit, 18-compile-time-mechanics.md line 210 (a Dionne talk in §18.8 references). grep -n -i -E 'fixed_string|template *< *auto|NTTP|non-type template|consteval.*pars' → 0 code hits; the only mention is one clause in Ch. 18 §18.4.1 ('a field offset passed as a template non-type parameter'). grep -n -i -E 'schema.*(generat|code)|sbe-tool|code ?generat' → 53-zero-copy-wire-market-data-decoding.md line 41 ('SBE … provide schema-driven code generators … use them rather than hand-writing offset arithmetic') and appendix-J line 58 — i.e. the book delegates to an external generator. Ch. 18 §18.1 line 15 explicitly promises 'a SBE/protocol decoder whose field layout is resolved at compile time (Ch. 53)' and §18.4.2 line 160 lists 'Protocol/decoding tables (Ch. 53). Field offsets and lengths for a fixed-layout message' — but neither chapter delivers it: Ch. 53's decode_binary (lines 79-96) uses literal offsets p+1, p+9, p+18, p+30 and §53.5 (lines 185-196) only verifies the memcpy+bswap lowering. No schema→constexpr table, no consteval field-list parse, no generated accessors, no static_assert against schema version, no reflection.

## G43 — The cost model of modern C++ idioms on the hot path: lambdas as closure types, std::ranges algorithms and views pipelines, std::invoke/apply/tuple, string_view/optional/expected, and strings without allocation (fixed-width symbols as integers, fixed-capacity strings, interning)

- **Theme:** E. Modern C++ depth · **Proposed kind:** new-chapter · **Sources:** cpp-features#3, algorithms-numerics#10
- **Verdict:** partially-covered · **Priority:** high
- **Why this priority:** Author-requested language-features area; intermediate-to-advanced readers write ranges/views/lambdas/string_view by default and the book currently gives no cost model or measurement for any of them, while symbol handling without allocation is on every feed-handler and gateway path.
- **Refined placement:** Split in two. (a) Strings: Ch. 25 new §25.4.6 'Strings without allocation: fixed-width symbols as integers, `string_view`, fixed-capacity strings, interning' (memcpy/bit_cast a `char[8]` into `uint64_t`, one `cmp`, one-multiply hash; libstdc++ 15 / libc++ 22-char SSO; `string_view` lifetime over the receive buffer; symbol-ID interning at startup), plus a §25.3 measurement row (symbol compare/lookup: `std::string` vs `string_view` vs `uint64` key) and a §25.5 pitfall; cross-reference from Ch. 53 §53.2.2 (`char sym[8]`), Ch. 46 §46.4.2 (symbol→info maps) and Ch. 71 (log arguments). (b) Lambdas as closure types (capture layout, Ch. 9), generic-lambda instantiation count, `std::invoke`/`std::apply`/`std::tuple` codegen, `std::ranges` algorithms vs raw loops (projections, iterator/sentinel pairs, when the sentinel defeats the vectorizer — `-Rpass-missed=loop-vectorize`), views pipelines (`filter`/`transform`/`take`/`zip`), `optional`/`expected`/`string_view` by value: a new Part III chapter after Ch. 20, 'The Cost of Modern Idioms', if the author accepts renumbering (do it together with G42); otherwise Ch. 20 new §20.4.4 (lambdas and callables) + §20.4.5 (ranges and views) with a §20.3 measurement row and §20.5 asm, and Appendix H rows for `<ranges>`/views availability.
- **What is missing / scope notes:** Covered only: std::string hides an allocation past SSO (a few sentences + an exercise). Missing: closure-type layout and inlining mechanics, generic lambdas, invoke/apply/tuple codegen, ranges algorithms vs loops, views pipelines and vectorization, string_view/optional/expected pass-by-value cost, fixed-width symbols as integers, fixed-capacity strings, interning. Every claim needs the §N.5 asm diff per the book's convention.
- **Closest existing coverage:**
  - 23-memory-management-fundamentals.md §23.1 (line 15), §23.4.1 (line 121), §23.5 (line 135), exercise 2 (line 150): std::string growth/SBO threshold as a hidden allocation, with an operator-new instrumentation exercise
  - 25-hotpath-hostile-stl-cache-friendly-containers.md §25.4.3 (line 137): std::string SBO and small-vector types; §25.2 (line 29) std::function in containers
  - 14-indirect-calls-virtual-dispatch.md §14.2.3 (line 66): 'prefer a concrete callable type (a template parameter)' — the lambda-vs-std::function conclusion without the closure-type mechanism
- **Evidence (greps and reads):** grep -n -i -E 'std::ranges|views::|<ranges>|ranges::|range-v3' chapters/*.md → 1 hit (18-compile-time-mechanics.md line 163, a parenthetical). grep -n -i -E '\blambda|closure' → 12-instruction-cache-frontend-code-layout.md line 157 (bloat), 14-indirect-calls-virtual-dispatch.md line 124 (benchmark code), 23-memory-management-fundamentals.md lines 15/135/150 ('a lambda returned by value'/'lambda capture' as hidden allocation), appendix-B line 41 (Java) — nothing on closure layout or why a template-parameter lambda inlines. grep 'std::invoke|std::apply' → 0; 'std::tuple' → Ch. 9 EBO layout only; 'string_view' → 0 across all 89 files; 'std::optional' → 1 (20-the-cost-of-abstractions.md line 144, as the C++17 fallback for expected); 'SSO|small.string|intern(ing|ed)|symbol.*uint64|char sym' → only the `char sym[8]` field in 53-zero-copy-wire-market-data-decoding.md line 82, never discussed as a key. The one developed angle: `std::string` as a hidden allocation with an SBO threshold — 23-memory-management-fundamentals.md §23.1 line 15, §23.4.1 line 121, §23.5 line 135, exercise 2 line 150 (instrument operator new on a std::string loop), 25-hotpath-hostile-stl-cache-friendly-containers.md §25.4.3 line 137 (SBO/small-vector), 71-zero-overhead-logging.md lines 60/104/117 (no std::string on the log path).

## G44 — Value categories, copy elision and the calling convention: SysV x86-64 classification (INTEGER/SSE/MEMORY, ≤16-byte aggregates in registers), why a non-trivial destructor forces pass-by-invisible-reference, guaranteed elision, [[clang::trivial_abi]]

- **Theme:** E. Modern C++ depth · **Proposed kind:** new-section-in-existing-chapter · **Sources:** cpp-features#4
- **Verdict:** genuinely-missing · **Priority:** high
- **Why this priority:** Author-requested language-features area; whether a 16-byte Order or an expected<Fill,Err> travels in registers or through memory is decided by the type's special members, affects every strong type and error return on the tick-to-trade path, and the book currently never explains it.
- **Refined placement:** Two small pieces, no new chapter. (1) Ch. 4 §4.2.1: add two paragraphs after the register table — SysV classification (INTEGER/SSE/MEMORY; aggregates ≤16 bytes travel in up to two registers; a non-trivial copy/move constructor or destructor forces pass/return by hidden pointer; >16 bytes → memory/`sret`) so readers can map asm of `f(Order)` or an `expected<Fill,Err>` return to the signature. (2) Ch. 20 new §20.4.4 'Passing and returning values: elision, moves and the ABI' — strong `Price`/`Qty`/`OrderId` types with defaulted special members that ride in registers, `unique_ptr<T>` by value vs `T*`, guaranteed prvalue elision vs NRVO, `[[clang::trivial_abi]]` and its ABI-break caveat, keeping `expected<T,E>` ≤16 bytes and trivially copyable; a §20.5 asm block (register vs hidden-pointer versions of the same call) and a §20.6 pitfall. Cross-reference Ch. 9. If G43's new chapter is created, §20.4.4 moves there as its mental-model section.
- **What is missing / scope notes:** Nothing beyond the register-role table exists. Note the Ch. 20 numbering collision with G46's proposed §20.4.4 — sequence them (§20.4.4 ABI/elision, §20.4.5 statics/TLS).
- **Closest existing coverage:**
  - 04-reading-the-machine-asm.md §4.2.1 (lines 33-38): SysV argument/return/callee-saved register table — prerequisite only, no aggregate classification
  - 20-the-cost-of-abstractions.md §20.2 (line 48), §20.4.1 (line 138), §20.4.2 (line 148): noexcept move ctors and 'keep E trivially copyable' — adjacent, no explanation of registers vs memory
  - 23-memory-management-fundamentals.md §23.4.1 (line 120): one clause on NRVO/return by value
- **Evidence (greps and reads):** grep -n -i -E 'copy elision|prvalue|xvalue|value categor|move semantic|move construct|trivial_abi|invisible reference|hidden reference|sret|INTEGER class|SSE class|MEMORY class|NRVO|\bRVO\b|calling convention' chapters/*.md → the only relevant hit is 23-memory-management-fundamentals.md line 120 ('Returning by value (NRVO/move) is cheap' — half a sentence); every 'elision/elided' hit is about bounds checks, HALO or dead benchmark code. grep 'SysV|System V' → 04-reading-the-machine-asm.md §4.2.1 lines 33-38 (register-role table: rdi/rsi/rdx/rcx/r8/r9 args, rax return, callee/caller-saved — no aggregate classification, no ≤16-byte rule, no hidden-pointer rule) and §4.7 line 242 (ABI reference); 50-inter-process-communication.md line 27 is SysV message queues. grep 'trivially.copyable' → 20-the-cost-of-abstractions.md lines 148/207/226 ('keep E small and trivially copyable so expected stays small' — no ABI reasoning), 21 (bit_cast precondition), 35/73 (seqlock payload). 'std::move' → only in the noexcept-move context of Ch. 20 lines 48/138 and Ch. 23 line 120.

## G45 — Object lifetime for zero-copy: implicit-lifetime types (P0593), std::start_lifetime_as / start_lifetime_as_array (C++23) as the sanctioned zero-instruction wire overlay, vs launder and bit_cast

- **Theme:** E. Modern C++ depth · **Proposed kind:** expand-existing-chapter · **Sources:** cpp-features#5
- **Verdict:** partially-covered · **Priority:** high
- **Why this priority:** Author-requested language-features area; the struct overlay is the hottest idiom in the system and the book's current guidance stops at per-field memcpy (and in one place suggests an overlay that is still UB), so the standard's sanctioned zero-instruction answer is a correctness-and-codegen gap on the tick-to-trade path.
- **Refined placement:** Expand Ch. 21: add an 'object lifetime vs storage' paragraph to §21.2 (P0593 implicit-lifetime types; which operations implicitly create objects — malloc, new unsigned char[], memcpy/memmove; why a NIC DMA ring, AF_XDP UMEM or mmap'd segment holds no `Msg` objects); rename §21.4.2 to '`std::bit_cast`, `std::launder` and `std::start_lifetime_as`' and add `start_lifetime_as<Msg>(buf)` / `start_lifetime_as_array` (C++23, P2590) as the zero-instruction sanctioned overlay with its preconditions (implicit-lifetime type, alignment, size) and the C++20 fallback (per-field memcpy, or `bit_cast` of the whole packed struct); §21.5 asm showing it emits nothing; §21.6 pitfall on misusing it for non-implicit-lifetime types or misaligned buffers. Re-point Ch. 53 §53.2.2 (line 37) and §53.6 (line 219) at it and remove/qualify the 'alignas(1) accessor' suggestion; cross-ref from Ch. 26/50 shared-memory overlays and fix Ch. 24's `reinterpret_cast<void**>` free list. Add an Appendix H row (libstdc++/libc++ status to be verified in the Phase-4 pass; fallback memcpy).
- **What is missing / scope notes:** Covered: bit_cast/memcpy/launder punning with codegen proof. Missing: the object-lifetime model (P0593), implicit-lifetime types, `std::start_lifetime_as`/`_array`, preconditions, and the consistency fix in Ch. 53/24. Toolchain support is thin — the Appendix H row must say so and the chapter must keep the memcpy path as the shipping fallback.
- **Closest existing coverage:**
  - 21-aliasing-restrict-type-punning.md §21.4.2 (lines 115-136): bit_cast / memcpy / launder for punning; §21.5 (lines 172-183) verified zero-cost asm; §21.6 (lines 188-193) pitfalls
  - 53-zero-copy-wire-market-data-decoding.md §53.2.2 (line 37), §53.5 (lines 185-196), §53.6 (line 219): per-field memcpy overlay with verified mov+bswap codegen
  - 24-custom-allocators.md §24.2 (line 54), §24.5 (line 173): storage vs object lifetime and launder on slot reuse, one bullet each
- **Evidence (greps and reads):** grep -n -i -E 'start_lifetime|implicit.lifetime|implicitly creat|P0593|P2590|construct_at|destroy_at|uninitialized_' chapters/*.md → 0. grep 'object lifetime|lifetime of' → 24-custom-allocators.md §24.2 line 54 (one bullet: allocators manage storage, placement-new constructs objects) and §24.5 line 173 (launder on slot reuse). The developed angle is value punning: 21-aliasing-restrict-type-punning.md §21.4.2 lines 115-136 (`std::bit_cast`, fixed-size `memcpy`, `std::launder` for storage reuse only, union punning UB) with verified asm in §21.5 lines 172-183 (vmovd) and pitfalls §21.6 lines 188-193; 53-zero-copy-wire-market-data-decoding.md §53.2.2 line 37 (naive `reinterpret_cast<Msg*>(buf)` is strict-aliasing UB and may be misaligned → memcpy per field 'or a carefully-alignas(1)/packed accessor'), §53.5 lines 185-196 (memcpy+bswap → mov+bswap), §53.6 line 219. The lifetime model is absent: nothing says a DMA/`mmap`/`recv` buffer contains no `Msg` object, nothing names implicit-lifetime types or `std::start_lifetime_as`, and the §53.2.2 'packed accessor' overlay suggestion is itself UB under the lifetime rules. 24-custom-allocators.md lines 84-89 use `*reinterpret_cast<void**>(p)` in the pool free list. appendix-H has no row for P2590.

## G46 — Hidden runtime in statics: function-local statics (__cxa_guard_acquire), thread_local and TLS models (__tls_get_addr vs initial-exec), static initialization order across TUs and dlopen'ed strategies, startup cost

- **Theme:** E. Modern C++ depth · **Proposed kind:** new-section-in-existing-chapter · **Sources:** cpp-features#6
- **Verdict:** genuinely-missing · **Priority:** medium
- **Why this priority:** Valuable and author-area (language-features) but contained: a few paragraphs fix it, and the hot-path hazards (per-call guard check, __tls_get_addr in a dlopen'd strategy, first-call init stall) are specialised codegen details rather than central architecture.
- **Refined placement:** Ch. 20 new §20.4.5 'Statics, `thread_local` and initialization: what the compiler emits' (numbered after G44's §20.4.4): function-local statics — guard byte, `__cxa_guard_acquire`, the acquire-load-and-branch paid on every call, `-fno-threadsafe-statics`, replacement by `constinit`/`constexpr` globals or explicit startup init; dynamic initialization order across TUs (`.init_array`, `[[gnu::init_priority]]`/`[[gnu::constructor]]`, inline variables) and inside a `dlopen`'d strategy .so; `thread_local` cost model — local-exec `fs:`-relative load in the main executable vs `__tls_get_addr` (plus first-touch per-thread allocation) in a shared object, `-ftls-model=initial-exec`, static linking. §20.5 asm pair (`call __cxa_guard_acquire` / `call __tls_get_addr` vs `mov rax, fs:[off]`), §20.6 pitfall. Cross-reference Ch. 18 §18.4.1, Ch. 33 §33.4.2, Ch. 46 §46.4.2 (the 'lazily-initialized state' is exactly this), Ch. 71/24 (per-thread logger/pool handles), Ch. 73 §73.4.4 and Ch. 74 §74.5. Alternative home if Ch. 20 grows too large: Ch. 23 §23.4.3.
- **What is missing / scope notes:** Only passing mentions exist. Keep the footprint to one section plus one asm block; the dlopen'd-.so TLS cost and first-call guard stall are the parts with direct hot-path consequence.
- **Closest existing coverage:**
  - 18-compile-time-mechanics.md §18.2 (line 41), §18.4.1 (line 132): constinit avoids static-init cost/order fiasco — two bullets, no emitted-code view
  - 33-false-sharing-thread-safety-anomalies.md §33.4.2 (line 132): thread_local recommended for per-thread data, with a parenthetical cost warning
