## G47 — Appendix: attribute, builtin and optimizer-hint reference (likely/unlikely, expect_with_probability, assume/unreachable, pure/const, hot/cold, flatten, target_clones, musttail, C++26 contracts) and an undefined-behaviour catalogue for performance programmers (what each UB rule buys the optimizer; cost of -fwrapv/-fno-strict-aliasing/-fno-delete-null-pointer-checks/-ftrivial-auto-var-init)

- **Theme:** E. Modern C++ depth · **Proposed kind:** new-appendix · **Sources:** cpp-features#7, cpp-features#10
- **Verdict:** partially-covered · **Priority:** medium
- **Why this priority:** Reference/consolidation material: the hot-path-critical attributes are already taught and measured in Ch. 12/21, so what is missing is the long tail of hints and the cost of UB-disabling flags — valuable to an advanced reader but not central to building the system.
- **Refined placement:** One new appendix after Appendix D (not two): 'Appendix M — Attributes, Builtins & What the Optimizer Assumes'. M.1 is the per-function companion to D's per-build flags: a table of attribute/builtin → what it tells the optimizer → GCC/Clang spelling → the chapter that measures it → how to verify; rows for `[[likely]]`/`__builtin_expect`/`__builtin_expect_with_probability` and how `-fprofile-use` overrides them, `[[gnu::hot/cold]]`/`noinline`/`always_inline`/`flatten`/`section`, `[[gnu::pure]]`/`[[gnu::const]]`, `[[assume]]`/`__builtin_assume`/`__builtin_unreachable`/`std::unreachable`, `std::assume_aligned`/`__builtin_assume_aligned`, `target_clones`/`__builtin_cpu_supports`/ifunc, `[[clang::musttail]]`, `[[nodiscard]]`/`[[noreturn]]`/`[[no_unique_address]]`, and C++26 contracts (pre/post/contract_assert; ignore/observe/enforce/quick_enforce; GCC 15 experimental). M.2 is the UB catalogue for performance programmers: UB rule → optimization it licenses → flag that buys it back (`-fwrapv`/`-fno-strict-overflow`/`-ftrapv`, `-fno-strict-aliasing`, `-fno-delete-null-pointer-checks`, `-ftrivial-auto-var-init=zero`, UBSan minimal-runtime/`-fsanitize-trap`) → measured cost on an existing example (Ch. 27 scaled-price loop, Ch. 21 axpy, Ch. 53 decode) → sanctioned replacement (`__builtin_*_overflow`, `std::add_sat`, `bit_cast`, `start_lifetime_as`). Add the flag rows to Appendix D.4 with one-line costs, and pointers from Ch. 12 §12.4, Ch. 21 §21.6, Ch. 40 §40.2, Ch. 72 §72.2.2 and Appendix D.5/D.7.
- **What is missing / scope notes:** Developed already: hot/cold/likely/inlining attributes (Ch. 12), strict-aliasing UB (Ch. 21), UBSan list and UB-as-vulnerability framing (Ch. 40/72). Missing: pure/const, expect_with_probability, target_clones/musttail, unreachable as an optimizer hint, contracts, and every measured flag cost (-fwrapv, -fno-strict-aliasing, -fno-delete-null-pointer-checks, -ftrivial-auto-var-init). Reference format fits an appendix; measurements should reuse existing chapter examples rather than new benchmarks.
- **Closest existing coverage:**
  - 12-instruction-cache-frontend-code-layout.md §12.4.1-12.4.2 (lines 86-188) and §12.5 (lines 209-248): hot/cold/noinline/always_inline/flatten/[[likely]]/__builtin_expect/section placement with verified output — the strongest existing treatment

## G48 — Non-owning callables and cheap type erasure: std::function_ref (C++26), std::move_only_function/copyable_function, in-place functions, std::any — measured against std::function and template parameters

- **Theme:** E. Modern C++ depth · **Proposed kind:** expand-existing-chapter · **Sources:** cpp-features#8
- **Verdict:** partially-covered · **Priority:** medium
- **Why this priority:** The hot-path answer the book already gives (template parameter or function pointer) is correct; the missing types mainly improve ergonomics and safety at the boundaries (event-loop completions, strategy hooks), so this is a worthwhile but specialised expansion of an existing measured section.
- **Refined placement:** Expand Ch. 14, no new chapter: extend §14.2.3 with the type-erasure design space (owning vs non-owning, SBO size, invoke thunk), add §14.4.5 'Lighter type erasure: `function_ref`, `move_only_function`/`copyable_function`, in-place functions' — `std::function_ref` (C++26, P0792) as a two-word non-owning object-pointer + thunk with one indirect call and zero allocation, the dangling-temporary hazard and `nontype_t` for free/member functions; `std::move_only_function` (C++23) / `copyable_function` (C++26) and what they fix (const/noexcept/ref qualifiers, no copy requirement) and don't (may still allocate); fixed-capacity `inplace_function<Sig, N>`; `std::any` as the anti-pattern. Add `function_ref` and `inplace_function` rows to the §14.3 benchmark, a §14.5 asm snippet (single `call [reg]`, no manager hop), a §14.6 pitfall (dangling `function_ref`) and a checklist line; Appendix H rows (`function_ref` not yet shipped — fallback `tl::function_ref`/`llvm::function_ref`; `move_only_function` libstdc++ 12, libc++ status to verify). Cross-reference from Ch. 47/48 completion callbacks and Ch. 73 §73.4.4 strategy hooks.
- **What is missing / scope notes:** Covered: why std::function is expensive, measured. Missing: every non-owning/non-allocating alternative (function_ref, move_only_function/copyable_function, inplace_function), their cost relative to std::function and a template parameter, and their toolchain availability.
- **Closest existing coverage:**
  - 14-indirect-calls-virtual-dispatch.md §14.2.3 (lines 58-66): std::function mechanism; §14.3 (lines 72-148): measured vs CRTP/virtual; §14.6 (lines 266-267), exercise 5 (line 284), checklist (line 292)
  - 19-template-metaprogramming-zero-cost.md §19.3 (lines 62-125): std::function row in the zero-cost benchmark
  - 25-hotpath-hostile-stl-cache-friendly-containers.md §25.2 (line 29), §25.5 (line 163): containers of std::function
  - 39-std-execution-senders-receivers.md §39.6 (lines 57-63, 103): type-erased senders collapse to std::function cost
- **Evidence (greps and reads):** grep -n -i -E 'function_ref|move_only_function|copyable_function|inplace_function|function_view|nontype_t|std::any\b|any_invocable' chapters/*.md → 0 in all files including Appendix H (rows 23-76 list no callable types). `std::function` is genuinely developed: 14-indirect-calls-virtual-dispatch.md §14.2.3 lines 58-66 (double indirection via manager/invoker, SBO ~2 pointers then heap allocation, inlining blocked, per-callable instantiation bloat), §14.3 lines 72-148 (four-way microbenchmark with a std::function row), §14.6 lines 266-267, exercise 5 line 284 (find the SBO threshold by instrumenting operator new), checklist line 292; 19-template-metaprogramming-zero-cost.md §19.3 lines 62-125 (std::function row in zerocost.cpp); 25-hotpath-hostile-stl-cache-friendly-containers.md §25.2 line 29, §25.5 line 163, checklist 186 (vector<std::function>); 39-std-execution-senders-receivers.md §39.6 lines 57/63/103 (type-erased any_sender collapses to std::function cost); 12 lines 157/263 (footprint). The alternatives offered are only 'a concrete callable type (a template parameter), a function pointer, std::variant+visitor, or CRTP', plus 'a hand-rolled void* + fn-ptr' named as another type-erased callback (§14.2.3 line 66). grep 'callback' in 38/47/48/73/74 → no callable type is ever prescribed for event-loop completions or strategy hooks.

## G49 — Build-time hygiene and build systems: template instantiation cost, extern template, PCH, unity builds, C++20 modules; CMake+Ninja vs Bazel/Buck2, hermetic pinned toolchains (Nix/containers), reproducible builds, remote caching, one flag policy without ODR/ABI drift

- **Theme:** E. Modern C++ depth · **Proposed kind:** new-chapter · **Sources:** cpp-features#9, sw-engineering#3
- **Verdict:** partially-covered · **Priority:** high
- **Why this priority:** It sits in the author-requested language-features area (modules, extern template, PCH), and the toolchain-pinning / one-flag-policy half is the missing mechanism behind outages Ch. 22 already warns about (-march SIGILL, ODR drift, stale PGO) and the reproducible builds Ch. 72 requires but never demonstrates.
- **Refined placement:** New chapter closing Part III, directly after Ch. 22 (new Ch. 23 'Build Systems, Build-Time Hygiene & Hermetic Toolchains'), ~3,500 words. §.3 measures the build like a latency path: -ftime-trace + ClangBuildAnalyzer and GCC -ftime-report on a template-heavy decoder/OrderBook<Policy> TU, cold vs ccache/sccache vs remote-cache wall time, and a bit-for-bit reproducibility diff of two builds. §.4 techniques: one flag policy via CMake presets/per-target options or Bazel/Buck2 toolchains, with deliberate per-TU -O3/-march overrides that cannot cause ODR/ABI drift (making Ch. 22 §22.5 concrete); explicit instantiation + extern template, PCH, unity builds and their ODR/anonymous-namespace pitfalls, C++20 modules and import std with current GCC/Clang/CMake support; hermetic pinned toolchains (Nix or containers; vendored GCC/Clang + libstdc++/libc++ + binutils/lld + target glibc); reproducible builds (SOURCE_DATE_EPOCH, -frandom-seed, -ffile-prefix-map, build-id) that make §72.4.7 actionable. Ch. 22 keeps its runtime-flag thesis and gains one forward pointer; App D.8 points at the chapter; App I gains an I.10 'Build & toolchain' one-liner section; App H gains a modules / import std row. Do not grow Ch. 22 in place: it is 3,328 words about runtime speed, and the two halves (language-level build hygiene; build-system/hermeticity/reproducibility) together are a chapter's worth.
- **What is missing / scope notes:** If a new chapter is refused, the smallest acceptable fallback is: language-level hygiene (extern template, PCH, unity, modules, -ftime-trace) as a new §22.4.4 with a §22.3 build-time measurement block, and build-system choice/hermetic toolchains/reproducible builds as a short new appendix. But the 'one flag policy without ODR/ABI drift' mechanism needs the build-system half, so splitting weakens both; prefer the chapter. Note also that two of the book's own warnings (Ch. 22 §22.5 -march=native SIGILL, stale PGO profiles; Ch. 72 §72.4.7 reproducibility) are build-system problems the book never shows how to prevent.
- **Closest existing coverage:**
  - 18-compile-time-mechanics.md §18.3 (build-time axis measured with /usr/bin/time and size), §18.5 (compile-time blowup pitfall; 'measure build time as a first-class metric')
  - 19-template-metaprogramming-zero-cost.md §19.5 and §19.6 (-ftime-trace / bloaty as verification of the build/size axis; exercise 4)
  - 22-build-toolchain-for-speed.md §22.4.2 (ThinLTO vs full LTO link cost), §22.5 (flag-drift/ODR pitfall and 'centralize the optimization flag policy'), §22.6 checklist
  - 72-secure-programming-low-latency.md §72.4.7 (reproducible and signed builds as a requirement, one bullet, no mechanism)
  - appendix-D-compiler-flag-reference.md D.8 (representative hot-path build line, flags only)
- **Evidence (greps and reads):** Greps across chapters/*.md: 'cmake|bazel|buck2|meson|\bninja\b' -> 0; 'ccache|sccache|remote cach|distcc|icecc' -> 0; 'unity build|jumbo|precompiled|\bPCH\b|extern template|explicit instantiation|-fno-implicit-templates' -> 0; 'import std|named module|header unit|-fmodules|C\+\+20 modules|\bBMI\b' -> only BMI = Bit Manipulation Instructions (Ch. 28, App D/H), modules 0; '\bNix\b|hermetic|SOURCE_DATE_EPOCH|reproducible build' -> Ch. 22 L147 (checklist: fixed -march 'for reproducible builds') and Ch. 72 L133 (one bullet: 'Pin the compiler and flags'); 'ftime-trace|ftime-report|ClangBuildAnalyzer|include-what-you-use' -> Ch. 18 L211 (reference pointer only), Ch. 19 L215/239/250 (verify the build/size axis with -ftime-trace/bloaty: an exercise and a checklist line); 'ODR|flag drift' -> Ch. 22 §22.5 L131, one pitfall bullet ('Letting flags drift across the codebase... Centralize the optimization flag policy'). Read Ch. 18 §18.3 (L53-118): a real build-time-vs-runtime measurement with /usr/bin/time + size on a constexpr table, and §18.5 L172 'measure build time as a first-class metric'. Ch. 22 §22.4.2 covers ThinLTO link-time cost; §22.8 points at lld/mold docs. App D.8 is a flags-only build line; App I (I.1-I.9) has no build/toolchain section. No build system is ever named; no toolchain pinning, caching, PCH/unity/extern-template, or modules content exists.

## G50 — C++20/23/26 library additions for the hot path: atomic_ref on wire/shm structs, atomic wait/notify and atomic_flag::test, 16-byte CAS and libatomic traps; std::inplace_vector/std::hive/static_vector; std::simd and portable SIMD wrappers

- **Theme:** E. Modern C++ depth · **Proposed kind:** expand-existing-chapter · **Sources:** cpp-features#12, cpp-features#13, cpp-features#14
- **Verdict:** partially-covered · **Priority:** high (was low)
- **Why this priority:** It is in the author-requested language-features area, and the atomics sub-item is a genuine hot-path correctness/latency trap in designs the book already ships (a 'lock-free' 16-byte CAS that takes a hidden libatomic lock; formally racy seqlock/shm payloads that atomic_ref fixes); the std::simd and inplace_vector parts are lower-stakes and should stay short.
- **Refined placement:** Expand existing chapters; no new chapter. Ch. 30: new §30.4.3 'Beyond std::atomic<T>: atomic_ref, wait/notify, and wide atomics' with a §30.3 measurement (16-byte CAS with and without -mcx16 -> cmpxchg16b vs the libatomic lock; std::atomic<T>::wait wake latency vs busy-poll, tying Ch. 32/41) and a §30.5 asm check; add a two-line static_assert(is_always_lock_free) discipline to Ch. 34 §34.4.3 (tagged-pointer DWCAS) and Ch. 50 §50.4.2 (atomics placed in shared memory). Ch. 35 §35.4.2: a short atomic_ref code sample for the seqlock payload read instead of a new section. Ch. 25: rename §25.4.3 'Intrusive, fixed-capacity and small-buffer containers' and fold in std::inplace_vector (C++26) with today's static_vector/etl equivalents and the capacity-branch cost. Ch. 29: new §29.4.4 'std::simd and portable wrappers' with one worked kernel (scalar vs std::simd vs Highway vs intrinsics) and a §29.5 codegen check. App H: rows for std::atomic<T>::wait/notify, std::inplace_vector, <simd>.
- **What is missing / scope notes:** Order of value inside the item: the atomics half first, because the book's own designs walk into the traps it would close (Ch. 34's tagged-pointer DWCAS silently becomes a libatomic lock without -mcx16 or on a misaligned 16-byte object; Ch. 35/50 plain-struct seqlock and shm payloads are formally racy and atomic_ref is the standard fix the text names but never shows). std::simd (C++26, with std::datapar naming churn in late drafts) and std::inplace_vector (C++26) are forward-looking: keep them brief, pair with the today-equivalents the book already names, and gate on feature-test macros per App H.6.
- **Closest existing coverage:**
  - 30-cpp-memory-model-atomics.md §30.6 (hidden-lock pitfall: check is_lock_free / is_always_lock_free) and §30.7 checklist
  - 34-lock-free-data-structures.md §34.4.3 (DWCAS / cmpxchg16b / casp named for tagged pointers; prose only)
  - 35-seqlocks-single-writer-publication.md §35.5 (atomic_ref as the fix for the seqlock data-race formality, one sentence)
  - 32-spinlocks-backoff-contention-control.md §32.2 and 41-context-switching-mitigation.md §41.3 (futex mechanism and measured wake cost that underlie C++20 atomic wait/notify, API not named)
  - 25-hotpath-hostile-stl-cache-friendly-containers.md §25.4.3 (SBO / small-vector bullet)
  - 29-simd-vectorization.md §29.4.2 (portable-wrapper recommendation: std::experimental::simd, Highway, xsimd, Eve) and §29.7 checklist
  - appendix-H-cpp-feature-matrix.md H.4 (std::atomic_ref and std::hive rows with trading-relevance note)
- **Evidence (greps and reads):** Greps across chapters/*.md: 'atomic_ref' -> Ch. 35 §35.5 L157 (one sentence: make the speculative seqlock read well-defined with relaxed atomics or atomic_ref), Ch. 35 L191 reference, App H L57 row + L63 'trading relevance' paragraph; no code anywhere. '\.wait\(|notify_one|notify_all|atomic_flag|\.test\(\)' -> 0 in all chapters; Ch. 32 §32.2 L54 covers futex internals and Ch. 41 §41.3 L58-88 measures a raw syscall(SYS_futex) ping-pong, Ch. 37 §37.4.2 L147 names blocking wait strategies 'a condition variable' — the C++20 std::atomic::wait/notify and atomic_flag::test API is never named. 'is_always_lock_free|is_lock_free|mcx16|libatomic|cmpxchg16b|DWCAS|__int128' -> Ch. 30 §30.6 L175 one pitfall bullet + checklist L197; Ch. 34 §34.4.3 L139/L142 names DWCAS/cmpxchg16b/casp for tagged pointers in prose; Ch. 33 L147 (padding can change is_lock_free); 0 for -mcx16, libatomic, __atomic_load_16; __int128 appears only in Ch. 27 notional math. 'inplace_vector|static_vector|std::hive|small_vector|SmallVector|InlinedVector|fixed.capacity' -> Ch. 25 §25.4.3 L137 one bullet (boost::small_vector / absl::InlinedVector / llvm::SmallVector, 'know the SBO threshold'), App H L61 std::hive row, Ch. 72 L90 names small_vector as an overflow-proof fixed-capacity type; inplace_vector / static_vector 0. 'std::simd|experimental::simd|simd_mask|element_aligned|basic_simd|datapar|Highway|xsimd|\bEve\b' -> Ch. 29 §29.4.2 L117 ('prefer a portable wrapper': std::experimental::simd, Highway, xsimd, Eve), checklist L195, references L203; App A.2 L34 same advice for NEON/SVE; App B L17 (Rust std::simd); no API shown, no code, no measurement; App H has no <simd> or inplace_vector row.

## G51 — The order-entry gateway and session layer: FIX session (Logon/Heartbeat/TestRequest/ResendRequest/SequenceReset/PossDup), SoupBinTCP, CME iLink 3/FIXP; sequence-number persistence and recovery; encode fast path; venue throttles; TCP liveness and failure-detection timing (keepalive, TCP_USER_TIMEOUT, tcp_retries2, RTO); reconnect and session takeover from a standby

- **Theme:** F. Trading-system components · **Proposed kind:** new-chapter · **Sources:** hft-domain#1, networking-depth#4, resilience-ha#3
- **Verdict:** partially-covered · **Priority:** high
- **Why this priority:** The gateway is on the tick-to-trade path (§76.3 budgets encode->TX at ~120 ns) and is the only component that can cancel working orders, yet the session layer, sequence recovery and TCP liveness timing that make it survivable have zero coverage — and two existing cross-references (Ch. 51 §51.4.1, App K.6) already point at content that does not exist.
- **Refined placement:** New chapter in Part XII immediately after Ch. 74 and before Ch. 75 ('The Order-Entry Gateway & Session Layer', ~3,800 words; prerequisites Ch. 51-53, 62.4.2, 74; leads into Ch. 75/76). Contents: session protocols as deterministic state machines (FIX session layer: Logon/Logout, Heartbeat/TestRequest, MsgSeqNum both directions, ResendRequest/SequenceReset-GapFill, PossDupFlag; SoupBinTCP under OUCH with requested sequence number; CME iLink 3/FIXP negotiation and retransmit; Cboe BOE) and why general-purpose FIX engines allocate and parse on the hot path; sequence-number persistence and recovery (journal next-in/next-out sequence numbers and in-flight orders to shm/WAL — Ch. 26/75 — so a crash-only restart (§74.2.4) resynchronizes without duplicating an order; idempotent ClOrdID); the encode fast path (pre-built message templates, to_chars into a preallocated frame, checksum, measured ns/encode against the §76.3 ~120 ns row); venue throttles and message budgets (K.4) in code; reconnect and session takeover from a standby gateway (who owns the sequence numbers, cancel-on-disconnect semantics, measured takeover time). Put the TCP failure-detection timing — SO_KEEPALIVE/TCP_KEEPIDLE/KEEPINTVL/KEEPCNT, TCP_USER_TIMEOUT, tcp_retries2 (~15 min to RST by default), RTO floor/RACK-TLP, thin-stream knobs, with a measured time-to-detect — as a new §52.4.4 in Ch. 52 ('Failure detection: how long until TCP tells you the gateway is gone'), where the socket knobs live and stalls are already measured; the gateway chapter cross-references it and layers application heartbeats/TestRequest on top. Fix the two dangling pointers: Ch. 51 §51.4.1 'FIX heartbeats — Ch. 53' and App K.6 '(J.3)' should point at the new chapter.
- **What is missing / scope notes:** Keep pre-trade risk and order-state reconciliation (separate proposals) out of this chapter beyond a hand-off. The chapter should measure three things the book currently never does: encode latency distribution, time-to-detect a dead peer under default vs tuned TCP timers, and resync/takeover time after a gateway restart.
- **Closest existing coverage:**
  - 74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.2.1 (gateway role: owns session, sequence numbers, working-order state) and §74.4.4 (cancel-on-disconnect / mass-cancel as the kill-switch mechanism)
  - appendix-J-market-structure-primer.md J.3 (order lifecycle state machine, order types, TIF, STP) and J.5 (OUCH/FIX message types)
  - appendix-K-exchange-connectivity.md K.4 (gateway ports, message-rate throttles, pre-trade-risk mandate) and K.6 (redundant gateways; one-line promise that sessions reconnect and resynchronize)
  - 53-zero-copy-wire-market-data-decoding.md §53.2.1 (OUCH/FIX/SBE encodings) and §53.4.1 (to_chars for outbound order fields)
  - 62-kernel-bypass-userspace-networking.md §62.4.2 (userspace TCP stacks for the order path and their correctness risk)
  - 46-keeping-the-hot-path-warm.md §46.4.2, 51-socket-optimization-tcp-tuning.md §51.4.1, 52-advanced-tcp-internals-tuning.md §52.4.1 (pre-established, heartbeated, warm order connections — speed, not liveness)
- **Evidence (greps and reads):** Greps across chapters/*.md: 'FIX engine|\blogon\b|\blogout\b|TestRequest|ResendRequest|SequenceReset|PossDup|MsgSeqNum|HeartBtInt|gap ?fill|FIXP|iLink|SoupBin|\bBOE\b|\bETI\b' -> only Ch. 53 L253 (reference list naming 'FIX and FIXP/SBE') and Ch. 54 L79 ('resend request' for market-data retransmission, not order entry); 'SO_KEEPALIVE|TCP_KEEPIDLE|keepalive|TCP_USER_TIMEOUT|tcp_retries2|tcp_syn_retries|\bRTO\b|rto_min|tail loss probe|RACK' -> 0 relevant hits (all matches are 'tracks'/'track'); 'session takeover|reconnect|standby|cancel.on.disconnect|heartbeat' -> Ch. 46 §46.4.2 L130-131/L164-166 (pre-establish and heartbeat connections, re-warm after reconnect), Ch. 51 §51.4.1 L98 ('keep connections warm with heartbeats (Ch. 46, FIX heartbeats — Ch. 53)' — Ch. 53 has no FIX session content, a dangling cross-reference), Ch. 74 §74.2.1 L37 (gateway 'owns the venue session: the TCP/order-entry connection, sequence numbers, working-order state'), §74.4.4 L127 (gateway 'can cancel-on-disconnect / mass-cancel the dead component's orders'), §74.4.3 (process heartbeats, not venue session heartbeats), App K.6 L57 ('sessions are designed to reconnect and resynchronize (J.3) without losing order state' — J.3 does not cover this). Read App J.3 (order lifecycle: types, TIF, New/Ack/Fill/Cancel state machine, STP), App K.4 (gateway ports, message-rate throttles, 15c3-5 pre-trade risk), Ch. 52 §52.4.1-52.4.3 (NODELAY/QUICKACK/NOTSENT_LOWAT/GRO/cc/userspace TCP — speed knobs only), Ch. 53 §53.2.1 and §53.4.1 (OUCH/FIX/SBE encodings; to_chars for outbound formatting), Ch. 62 §62.4.2 (userspace TCP for order entry, 'a TCP bug = a lost/duplicated order'), Ch. 76 §76.3 (encode->TX budgeted ~120 ns, Ch. 53/62). Nothing develops the session protocol state machine, sequence persistence/recovery, TCP failure-detection timing, throttle handling in code, or reconnect/takeover.

## G52 — Strategy engine and incremental signal computation: the pure on_event core, SoA per-instrument state, O(1) streaming statistics (Welford, EWMA with precomputed decay, rolling sums/min/max, order-flow imbalance), fixed-memory windows, floating-point drift and determinism

- **Theme:** F. Trading-system components · **Proposed kind:** new-chapter · **Sources:** hft-domain#5, algorithms-numerics#6
- **Verdict:** partially-covered · **Priority:** high
- **Why this priority:** The strategy stage is one of the five tick-to-trade stages and the one §76.3 identifies as the dominant p99.9 contributor, yet how its signals are computed in O(1), in fixed memory and deterministically has no chapter — 'alpha is out of scope' does not excuse leaving the computational mechanics untaught.
- **Refined placement:** New chapter closing Part V after Ch. 29 (new Ch. 30 'Streaming Statistics & Incremental Signal Computation on the Hot Path', ~3,500 words; prerequisites Ch. 8, 11, 27-29, 74). Keep the alpha itself out of scope as J.6 says, and teach the mechanics: §.2 the strategy as on_event(state, event) -> actions with per-instrument SoA state, hot/cold split and zero allocation, pointing to Ch. 74 §74.2.2 and Ch. 8 rather than re-deriving them; §.3 measure recompute-the-window vs O(1) incremental update (hundreds of ns vs tens), add-new/subtract-old drift over 1e9 updates vs periodic exact recompute, Kahan/Neumaier or integer accumulation, and the EWMA denormal cliff with and without FTZ/DAZ (Ch. 27); §.4 Welford/West online mean/variance/covariance, EWMA/EWMV with precomputed per-step decay and the irregular-time exp(-dt/tau) form via a table or polynomial (no exp on the hot path), rolling sums over power-of-two rings (Ch. 28 masks, Ch. 34 rings), rolling min/max via a monotonic deque, order-flow imbalance and microprice read straight off the Ch. 25 book, skipping recomputation when the update touches unused depth; §.5 codegen: the EWMA recurrence as a dependency chain (Ch. 11), FMA contraction and -ffp-contract for bit-exact replay; §.6 pitfalls: catastrophic cancellation in naive variance, reading the clock inside the core, denormals, float drift breaking deterministic replay (Ch. 74/75). Revise App J.6's 'outside this book's scope' line to point here, and add a pointer from Ch. 76 §76.2 for EWMA/streaming monitors in production.
- **What is missing / scope notes:** Do not fold this into Ch. 27 (already 4,206 words, about representation) — the streaming primitives are a chapter's worth and have their own measurement and codegen story. A short note on exponentially-decayed counters for rate limiting ties to the gateway throttle work in G51.
- **Closest existing coverage:**
  - 74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.2.2 (pure apply(state,input) core; time and randomness as inputs; replayability)
  - 08-cache-aware-data-oriented-design.md §8.3 and §8.4.3 (SoA per-level order-book arrays, measured; hot/cold field split)
  - 27-fixed-point-floating-point-arithmetic.md §27.2.3 (EWMA settling into denormals), §27.4.3 (cross-build determinism), §27.6 (Kahan named in one phrase)

## G53 — Quoting engine and market-making mechanics: quote-pull latency as the critical metric, one-tick-to-N-requotes fan-out across options chains, delta thresholds and hysteresis, mass quote/mass cancel, cancel-first discipline

- **Theme:** F. Trading-system components · **Proposed kind:** new-chapter · **Sources:** hft-domain#6
- **Verdict:** genuinely-missing · **Priority:** medium
- **Why this priority:** Market making is the dominant HFT business and quote-pull latency is a real metric the taker-side tick-to-trade framing never measures, but it is specialised (options/futures market making) and its mechanics depend on the gateway and strategy chapters existing first.
- **Refined placement:** New chapter, conditional on the surrounding chapters: if the G51 gateway chapter and G52 strategy chapter are adopted, add 'Quoting Engine & Market-Making Mechanics' directly after the gateway chapter (Part XII, or in a new 'Trading-System Components' Part grouping G51/G52/G53/G55), ~3,000 words: the quote lifecycle and cancel-first discipline; quote-pull latency (decision -> cancel on the wire) measured as a second critical metric beside tick-to-trade and added as a row to §76.3; one-tick -> N-requotes fan-out across an options chain / futures spreads / ETF baskets under a message budget, with prioritization, delta thresholds and hysteresis to prevent requote storms (ties Ch. 59's self-inflicted microburst and K.4 throttles); mass quote / mass cancel / purge-port semantics per venue (CME, Eurex, Cboe) and cancel-on-disconnect; the batch tier (GPU Greeks, Ch. 67) feeding seqlock/RCU-published vol and Greek tables (Ch. 35/73) that the hot path reads. Smallest-footprint fallback: fold pull latency, cancel-first and mass quote/cancel into the gateway chapter (G51), fan-out/delta thresholds/hysteresis into the strategy chapter (G52), and add the quote-pull row to §76.3 — do not create this chapter in isolation.
- **What is missing / scope notes:** The book's measurement framing is entirely taker-side (new order out); the maker-side metric (pull latency, i.e. decision to cancel-on-wire) is distinct and currently unmeasured. Options-chain fan-out is also the strongest real motivation in the book for Ch. 59's scheduled/paced transmission.
- **Closest existing coverage:**
  - appendix-J-market-structure-primer.md J.1 (mention only: HFT firms are typically market makers)
  - 74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.4.4 (mention only: mass-cancel as a kill-switch action)
  - 59-precise-scheduled-transmission.md §59.1 and §59.4.3 (TX mechanism only: self-inflicted microbursts, coordinated sends)
  - 67-gpu-computing-cuda.md §67.4.3 (Greeks/pricing on the off-hot-path batch tier)
- **Evidence (greps and reads):** Greps across chapters/*.md: 'market.mak|requote|re-quote|quote pull|pull(ing)? (the |a |our )?quotes?|pull latency|mass cancel|mass quote|cancel all|bulk cancel|purge|options? chain|two.sided|hysteresis|delta threshold|adverse selection|quote storm|cancel.first|cancel-replace|cancel/replace' -> App J.1 L18 ('An HFT firm is typically a market maker (quoting both sides, earning the spread/rebate)', one sentence), App J.3 L37 ('Cancel-Replace' named in the lifecycle), Ch. 74 §74.4.4 L127 ('cancel-on-disconnect / mass-cancel the dead component's orders' as a kill-switch tool); every other hit is 'two-sided RDMA' (Ch. 63, App F). Word-level '\bquot(e|es|ing)\b' appears at most twice per file and only generically (Ch. 1 'an exchange publishes a quote'). Related angles only: Ch. 67 §67.4.3 puts options pricing/Greeks on the GPU batch tier; Ch. 59 §59.1 (self-inflicted microbursts) and §59.4.3 (coordinated multi-venue sends) cover the TX mechanism a requote fan-out would use. No quote-pull latency metric, requote fan-out, delta thresholds/hysteresis, mass-quote message mechanics, or cancel-first discipline anywhere.

## G54 — Multi-venue book building, normalization, symbology and smart order routing; venue feed and session anatomy (MoldUDP64/SoupBinTCP, CME MDP 3.0 and iLink 3, Eurex EOBI/EMDI/ETI, Cboe PITCH/BOE, the SIP)

Keep it in Part IX, the market-data Part, because its inputs are the feed handler's outputs. ## G55 — Case study: the exchange simulator — a price-time CLOB matching engine, pro-rata variant, self-trade prevention modes, auctions, a latency model sampled from captured ack timestamps, and queue-position modelling

- **Theme:** F. Trading-system components · **Proposed kind:** new-chapter · **Sources:** hft-domain#8
- **Verdict:** partially-covered · **Priority:** medium
- **Why this priority:** The simulator is test infrastructure off the hot path, but the book relies on 'a simulation harness' in Ch. 53/54/75/76 exercises and the CI latency gates without ever building one, and a measured matching engine is also the venue-side proof of the order-book design.
- **Refined placement:** New case-study chapter in Part XII between Ch. 75 and Ch. 76 ('Case Study: The Exchange Simulator', ~3,500 words), so §75.4.5 and §76.4.3 can point at a built artefact instead of a requirements list. Contents: a single-threaded deterministic price-time CLOB matching engine reusing the Ch. 25 (or G56) book and the J.3 order types/TIF, a pro-rata allocation variant, self-trade-prevention modes, and an opening/closing auction cross, measured at millions of orders/s with the match loop's asm checked (Ch. 4); a latency model with per-participant one-way delay and jitter distributions sampled from captured ack timestamps (Ch. 58/75) and separate market-data dissemination vs order-ack delays; a queue-position model (your order's rank at a level under FIFO vs pro-rata, estimated from MBO data when available — ties G56); gap/A-B/microburst injection (Ch. 53/54) so the harness can drive the Ch. 76 tail conditions deterministically. Do not grow §76.4.3 in place — Ch. 76 is the capstone and should consume the simulator, not build it. If a chapter is refused, the minimum is expanding §76.4.3 with the matching core's code and the latency-model sampling method.
- **What is missing / scope notes:** The chapter also gives the book its first venue-side measurement of the order-book design (G56) and a concrete closed-loop test bed for the CI latency gates in §76.4.1; the pro-rata and auction variants can be brief, the latency and queue-position models are the parts most likely to turn a backtest into fiction and deserve the measurement budget.
- **Closest existing coverage:**
  - 76-production-profiling-end-to-end-case-study.md §76.4.3 (what a simulation harness must model — requirements only) and §76.6 exercise 5 / checklist
  - 75-capture-persistence-replay-storage.md §75.4.5 (simulation-harness bullet: fills, rejects, queue position)
  - appendix-J-market-structure-primer.md J.1 (price-time priority, pro-rata, auctions — definitions) and J.3 (order types, TIF, STP, order state machine)
  - 25-hotpath-hostile-stl-cache-friendly-containers.md §25.4.4 (price-time intrusive FIFO per level — the matching engine's own structure)
  - 74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.2.2 (deterministic single-threaded core pattern the simulator should follow)
- **Evidence (greps and reads):** Greps across chapters/*.md: 'matching engine' -> App J.1 L9-16 (CLOB, price-time priority, pro-rata, maker-taker, opening/closing auctions — ~200 words of definitions, no code), App F L19, Ch. 1/17/60/65 incidental ('the thing you race to'); 'pro.rata' and 'auction' -> App J.1 only; 'self.trade|\bSTP\b' -> App J.3 L38, one line; 'simulation harness|simulator|simulated (venue|exchange)' -> Ch. 76 §76.4.3 L84-91 (four bullets, ~230 words: model venue responses including queue position/fills/partials/rejects/latency, closed loop, inject rare/hostile conditions, complements replay), Ch. 76 ex. 5 L113 and checklist L124, Ch. 75 §75.4.5 L107 (one bullet), Ch. 76 L30 diagram, App A L76; 'queue.position' -> Ch. 75 L107 and Ch. 76 L88/L113 as something the harness 'models' — no model given; 'latency model|fill model|exchange simulator|venue simulator' -> 0. Ch. 25 §25.4.4 L152 describes the price-time FIFO level structure a matching engine would reuse (prose only); Ch. 74 §74.2.2 gives the single-threaded deterministic core pattern. ## G56 — Limit order book engineering in depth: market-by-price (L2) vs market-by-order (L3), dense tick-indexed arrays with re-centering vs sparse/sorted stores, order-ID maps, snapshot rebuild, BBO vs depth hot/cold split, measured on captured data

- **Theme:** F. Trading-system components · **Proposed kind:** new-chapter · **Sources:** algorithms-numerics#7
- **Verdict:** partially-covered · **Priority:** high
- **Why this priority:** The order book is the data structure on the tick-to-trade critical path that §76.3 identifies as the dominant tail contributor, the book's own case study is an unmeasured 700-word outline that violates its 'every claim backed by a measurement' rule, and L2-vs-L3 and dense-vs-sparse indexing decide memory and per-message cost for every feed handler.
- **Refined placement:** Promote §25.4.4 to a full chapter in Part IV directly after Ch. 25 (new Ch. 26 'Limit Order Book Engineering'; Memory Mapping shifts to Ch. 27), leaving a one-paragraph pointer in Ch. 25 §25.4.4. Prerequisites Ch. 8, 24, 25, 28; feeds Ch. 35, 53, 54, 74, 75/76 and the G55 simulator. §.2 market-by-price (L2 aggregates, no order IDs) vs market-by-order (L3 per-order add/modify/delete/execute with order-ID map + per-level FIFO) vs top-of-book feeds — memory per instrument and per-message work for each; price-level indexing designs with code: dense tick-indexed array with a sliding window and the re-centering/relocation event when the BBO walks off the edge (a tail event to design and measure), sorted small arrays with branchless/SIMD search for sparse options books, flat_map and bitmap-indexed hybrids (Ch. 28); order-ID map choices (open addressing keyed by venue-assigned IDs, generational slot indices). §.3 measured on captured ITCH/MBO data against the std::map<Price, std::list<Order>> baseline Ch. 25 already names: ns/update p50/p99.9, the re-centering tail, zero hot-path allocation verified. §.4 BBO vs depth hot/cold split, snapshot rebuild and increment splice (pointing to Ch. 54), BBO publication (Ch. 35), consolidated multi-venue attribution handed off to G54. §.5 codegen of the add/cancel fast path. §.6 pitfalls: tick-size changes and price bands breaking the dense index, crossed books during recovery, order-ID reuse across sessions, L2 feeds that hide queue position.
- **What is missing / scope notes:** Growing §25.4.4 in place would push Ch. 25 past ~6,000 words and bury the book's central data structure inside a containers chapter; a chapter also lets the simulator (G55) and strategy (G52) chapters build on one measured artefact. The chapter must supply the measurement the current text promises but omits.
- **Closest existing coverage:**
  - 25-hotpath-hostile-stl-cache-friendly-containers.md §25.4.4 (design outline: tick-indexed level array with BBO index, intrusive pool FIFO per level, flat order-ID map, BBO-vs-depth hot/cold split, consolidated feeds — prose only) and §25.6 exercise 4
  - 08-cache-aware-data-oriented-design.md §8.3 and §8.4.3 (SoA order-book level arrays, measured; point-update vs sweep tension)
  - 28-bit-manipulation-integer-tricks.md §28.4.1 (level-occupancy bitmaps: best level above touch via tzcnt, iterate via tzcnt+blsr)
  - 35-seqlocks-single-writer-publication.md §35.3-§35.4 (BBO snapshot publication, measured)
  - 54-reliable-multicast-feed-recovery.md §54.4.2 (snapshot rebuild and live-increment splice)
  - appendix-J-market-structure-primer.md J.2 (BBO/depth/NBBO definitions)
- **Evidence (greps and reads):** Read 25-hotpath-hostile-stl-cache-friendly-containers.md §25.4.4 in full (L140-156): 701 words of prose plus one ASCII diagram, no code, no benchmark; the performance claim '~hundreds of ns -> ~tens of ns per update' is unmeasured and exercise 4 (L176) asks the reader to build and measure it. Greps across chapters/*.md: 'market.by.order|market.by.price|\bMBO\b|\bMBP\b|order.by.order|L3 book|level 3' -> 0 (App J.2 L26 uses 'level 2, level 3' to mean depth levels, not feed types; App I's L2/L3 are caches); 'sparse (book|array|price|level)|tick.indexed|re.?cent(er|re)|price ladder' -> only §25.4.4's own phrases ('a ring/sliding array, or a dense array bounded by the instrument's plausible range'; deep levels in 'a secondary (sparse/flat_map) structure') with no mechanism for the window walking off the edge; 'order.ID map|orderID' -> §25.4.4 L152 and ex. 4 (named as a flat hash map); 'snapshot rebuild|rebuild the book' -> Ch. 54 §54.2 L31/L39, §54.4.2 L80-82, ex. 3 L110 (developed from the feed-recovery side: consume snapshot, buffer live increments, splice); 'BBO|top.of.book|depth' -> Ch. 8 §8.3/§8.4.3 L101-200 (SoA per-level arrays measured, 'full treatment in Ch. 25'), Ch. 28 §28.4.1 L92 and ex. 3 L183 (level-occupancy bitmap with tzcnt/blsr), Ch. 35 §35.3 L67-130 (BBO seqlock publication, measured), App J.2 (BBO/depth defined; 'drives data-structure choice (Ch. 24-25)').

## G57 — Encrypted and web-protocol venues and trading in the cloud: TLS/kTLS cost per record, WebSocket framing, JSON fast paths (simdjson), REST rate limits, FIX-over-TLS; virtualized NICs (ENA/EFA/SR-IOV), placement groups, cloud PTP, and the latency you cannot buy

- **Theme:** F. Trading-system components · **Proposed kind:** new-chapter · **Sources:** hft-domain#13, networking-depth#6, networking-depth#7
- **Verdict:** genuinely-missing · **Priority:** medium
- **Why this priority:** Valuable and growing (crypto/cloud venues are where much new low-latency work happens) but orthogonal to the book's stated bare-metal, binary-multicast, colocated framing; author area is 'other'.
- **Refined placement:** Two pieces, two placements. (1) New chapter in Part X immediately after Ch. 65 (Transport Beyond TCP) — 'Encrypted & Web-Protocol Venues: TLS/kTLS, WebSocket, JSON and REST on a latency budget' — as the closing transport chapter: Ch. 65 covers purpose-built fast transports, this covers the slow-by-mandate ones (TLS 1.3 handshake/resumption/0-RTT replay hazard for orders, AES-GCM vs ChaCha20 ns/record, kTLS vs OpenSSL/BoringSSL, WebSocket framing and masking, simdjson/on-demand JSON decode into the Ch. 53 overlay discipline, REST rate limits, FIX-over-TLS). Prefer Part X over the hft-domain#13 suggestion of Part VIII after Ch. 52, which would interrupt the kernel-stack→zero-copy progression before the binary-feed chapters. Builds on Ch. 29, 38, 46, 47–48, 52–54, 70. (2) Cloud: new Appendix M after Appendix K ('Trading in the Cloud: SR-IOV/ENA/EFA, placement groups, cloud PTP, and the latency you cannot buy'), cross-referenced from A.7, K.1 and Ch. 6; an appendix rather than a chapter because it is infrastructure/operational like K, and folding it into K would more than double a 1,838-word appendix. A chapter is justified for (1) because coverage is literally zero and the venue profile needs a mechanism + measurement treatment; a §-level insertion has no natural host chapter.
- **What is missing / scope notes:** Entirely absent, so the full proposed scope stands. Keep the chapter honest about the latency tier (100s of µs to ms) and what from Parts VIII–IX transfers (busy-poll, warming, zero-copy parse discipline) vs what does not (multicast, cross-connects, NIC timestamps on the venue side). Measurements needed: TLS record decrypt ns/record by cipher and library, kTLS vs userspace syscall/copy cost, WebSocket frame parse cost, simdjson vs nlohmann on a representative book-update message, TLS 1.3 resumption vs full handshake RTTs. Cloud appendix: ENA LLQ/interrupt-moderation knobs, SR-IOV VF vs virtio, DPDK on ENA, placement groups and instance-size queue counts, Amazon Time Sync/PTP-as-a-service accuracy vs Ch. 58, hypervisor jitter measured with cyclictest (ties Ch. 6/45), and the floor you cannot buy.
- **Closest existing coverage:**
  - 70-smartnics-dpus.md §70.4.2 (line 98): TLS/IPsec named as a DPU offload category — one phrase, no cost model
  - 29-simd-vectorization.md §29.7 and 53-zero-copy-wire-market-data-decoding.md §53.8: simdjson cited as a SIMD-parsing technique reference, never applied to a JSON venue
  - appendix-A-arm-graviton.md §A.7 (lines 72–77): one paragraph on where cloud fits (research tiers) vs bare-metal colocation; no virtualized-NIC or placement mechanics
  - 06-system-setup-for-low-latency.md §6.2.3 (line 56): cloud named only as the case where mitigations must stay on
  - 46-keeping-the-hot-path-warm.md §46.4.2 / 52-advanced-tcp-internals-tuning.md: TCP connection warming and cold-start — kernel TCP only, no TLS layer
- **Evidence (greps and reads):** Greps run across /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/*.md: 'ktls' → 0; '\bTLS\b' excluding thread-local → only 70-smartnics-dpus.md:98 (one phrase: 'inline encryption/decryption (TLS/IPsec)' as a DPU offload category); 'openssl|boringssl|wolfssl|rustls' → 0; '0-RTT|session resumption|early data' → 0; 'websocket' → 0; 'simdjson|\bJSON\b' → only reference-list entries (29-simd-vectorization.md:202, 53-zero-copy-wire-market-data-decoding.md:254) plus Google Benchmark JSON output in Appendix L; '\bREST\b|HTTPS?|rate.limit' → no HTTP/REST hits (only prose 'the rest'); 'binance|coinbase|crypto.?(venue|exchange)' → 0; '\bENA\b|elastic network adapter|\bEFA\b|placement group|nitro|SR-IOV|virtual function|\bVFs?\b' → 0; 'hypervisor|virtualization|virtual machine|KVM|xen|virtio|vhost|steal time' → 0 (only 'virtual dispatch' prose); 'AWS|EC2|azure|GCP|cloud' → Appendix A.7 (one paragraph: cloud is not colocated, no Solarflare/FPGA in EC2, Local Zones narrow the gap), 06-system-setup §6.2.3 line 56 (don't disable mitigations on a cloud box), 63-infiniband §63.2 line 92 (RoCE used in cloud MPI), 00-preface (Graviton pointer). 'ddos|anycast|reverse proxy|load balancer|handshake|SYN' → 0 on connection setup. Opened Ch. 46 §46.4.2 (pre-touching TCP connections) and Ch. 52 (TCP cold start) — kernel-TCP warming only, no TLS record/handshake cost. Nothing teaches TLS record cost, kTLS vs userspace, WebSocket framing, JSON fast paths, REST throttles, or virtualized-NIC/placement-group/cloud-PTP latency.

## G58 — Regulation as engineering requirements: SEC 15c3-5, MiFID II RTS 6 (algo controls, kill functionality) and RTS 25 (clock traceability), CAT and audit-trail design mapped to code paths and measurable requirements

- **Theme:** F. Trading-system components · **Proposed kind:** expand-existing-chapter · **Sources:** hft-domain#14
- **Verdict:** partially-covered · **Priority:** low
- **Why this priority:** Marginal to the C++/Linux theme and already named where it drives a design (pre-trade gate, PTP, kill-switch, retention); the remaining content is a requirements map best kept short in an appendix.
- **Refined placement:** Keep as an expansion of Appendix K: new §K.8 'Regulation that touches latency engineering' inserted before the current K.8 References (renumber to K.9/K.10). Not a chapter — the material is a requirements map, not a mechanism, and K already hosts 15c3-5 and RTS 25. Add one-line back-references from Ch. 58 §58.1 (RTS 25 tiers), Ch. 74 §74.4.4 (RTS 6 kill functionality and conformance testing), Ch. 75 §75.4.4 (CAT/RTS 25 record fields and retention) and Ch. 72 §72.4.7 (tamper-evidence of the record).
- **What is missing / scope notes:** Already present and adequate: 15c3-5 as a mandatory pre-trade gate on the path (K.4, J.3, 74), RTS 25 as the clock-sync/PTP driver with the 100 µs/1 µs figures (K.5, 58). Missing and worth adding: MiFID II RTS 6 as the control list it is (kill functionality living in a surviving process → Ch. 74 §74.4.4, pre-trade controls, mandatory conformance/stress testing of algos, algo identifiers on every order, annual self-assessment); CAT and order-event audit-trail design (which fields/timestamps must be captured per event, clock granularity, linkage keys, retention → Ch. 75); exchange order-to-trade and message-ratio throttles as engineering limits (ties K.4); and a short table mapping each rule to a code path, a measurable requirement and the chapter that satisfies it. Keep it jurisdiction-neutral and dated — regulation moves faster than the book.

## G59 — Post-trade latency analytics: joining capture, order lifecycle and venue timestamps to compute fill rate, markout and race outcomes as a function of latency — the marginal value of a nanosecond

- **Theme:** F. Trading-system components · **Proposed kind:** new-section-in-existing-chapter · **Sources:** observability-depth#10
- **Verdict:** genuinely-missing · **Priority:** medium
- **Why this priority:** It is the empirical justification for the book's north-star (latency wins fills) and closes a dangling forward reference, but it is post-trade analytics off the hot path rather than building the C++/Linux system itself.
- **Refined placement:** New section in Ch. 76 immediately after §76.4.3 Simulation harnesses — '§76.4.4 Closing the loop: from the latency ledger to fills, markouts and race outcomes' (numbered 76.4.4 if adopted alone; the source's §76.4.6 assumes sibling proposals observability-depth#7/#8 land first). A section, not an appendix: it is the natural consumer of §76.3's decomposition and gives Ch. 71 §71.1's forward reference a landing. Cross-reference Ch. 75 (capture + order journal as the join inputs), Ch. 58 §58.4.2 (venue timestamps), Appendix J.3 (order lifecycle states) and Appendix K.7. Also fix Ch. 71 §71.1's pointer to name the new section.
- **What is missing / scope notes:** Nothing exists, so the full scope applies: the join (triggering tick → decision → order TX hardware timestamp → venue ack/fill with venue timestamp) computed per order from Ch. 75's captures and Ch. 73's recorded config version; per-order reaction latency, time-to-ack, filled/not-filled, inferred queue position at entry; markout at +1/+10/+100 ms as the adverse-selection measure; fill-rate-vs-latency curves per symbol/venue with the knee beyond which faster buys nothing; race analysis (which competitor got there first, by how many ns, from venue timestamps); using the curve to decide which §76.3 stage to attack next. Keep the C++ angle: this is an off-hot-path batch job over binary journals (ties Ch. 71/75 decoders), and a worked example on a captured session with a plot of fill rate vs reaction latency. Do not drift into strategy design.
- **Closest existing coverage:**
  - appendix-K-exchange-connectivity.md §K.7 (line 64): one sentence asserting the marginal nanosecond changes fill rates — no method
  - 58-clock-synchronization-ptp.md §58.1 (line 13) and §58.4.2: 'did our quote or theirs arrive first' and 'position in the queue' as questions synced timestamps make answerable — not developed
  - 76-production-profiling-end-to-end-case-study.md §76.4.3 and 75-capture-persistence-replay-storage.md §75.4.5: simulation harness models queue position/fills (forward simulation, not realised-fill analysis)
  - 71-zero-overhead-logging.md §71.1 (line 15): dangling forward reference to 'post-trade analysis (Ch. 75–76)'
- **Evidence (greps and reads):** Greps across chapters/*.md: 'markout|mark-out|mark out|adverse select|slippage|\bTCA\b|transaction cost' → 0; 'fill.?rate|hit.?rat|fill probability' → appendix-K-exchange-connectivity.md:64 (one sentence: 'firms spend where the marginal nanosecond still changes fill rates') and 54-reliable-multicast-feed-recovery.md:108 ('A/B fill rate' — gap-fill by arbitration, a different meaning); 'race outcome|got there first|win the race|queue position|marginal (nano|micro)|value of a (nano|micro)|post.trade' → 58-clock-synchronization-ptp.md:13 (poses 'did our quote or theirs arrive first?' as a question PTP enables, never answered), 58 §58.4.2 line ~103 ('your position in the queue' as something comparable exchange timestamps allow — one clause), 75-capture §75.4.5 line 107 and 76 §76.4.3 lines 88/113 (simulation harness *models* queue position and fills — forward simulation, not analysis of realised fills vs latency), 71-zero-overhead-logging.md:15 (forward reference to 'post-trade analysis (Ch. 75–76)' that has no landing section). Opened Ch. 76 §76.3–§76.5 fully: the decomposition stops at per-stage latency; nothing joins the latency ledger to order outcomes. Appendix J.3 gives the order state machine (ack/fill/cancel) but no analytics on it.

## G60 — Testing latency-critical code: unit and table-driven tests of apply(state,input), property-based testing, deterministic simulation testing (DST) with injected faults, test doubles for the impure shell without hot-path cost, golden-master tests from captures

- **Theme:** G. Engineering lifecycle · **Proposed kind:** new-chapter · **Sources:** sw-engineering#1
- **Verdict:** partially-covered · **Priority:** high
- **Why this priority:** Central to building a tick-to-trade system: the book architects a 'pure, testable' deterministic core (Ch. 74) yet contains no test code and no method for testing order-book/arbitration/strategy logic beyond replay-equals-golden — the class of bug that actually loses money.
- **Refined placement:** A new chapter is justified: Part XII, after Ch. 75 (Capture, Persistence & Replay Storage) and before Ch. 76 (the capstone), titled along the lines of 'Testing the Deterministic Core: Unit, Property-Based and Deterministic Simulation Testing'. It needs Ch. 74's state-machine shape and Ch. 75's captures as inputs and feeds Ch. 76's gates, so this slot is right; Ch. 40 stays the dynamic/concurrency-tooling chapter and its 'Leads into' should name the new chapter as the logic-testing complement. A section inside Ch. 74 (§74.4.6) was considered and rejected: Ch. 74 is already the largest Part XII chapter (4,400 words) and the scope (test doubles as C++ design, property-based testing, DST with fault injection, golden masters) exceeds a section. If the G61 CI chapter is also adopted, the two could be merged into one 'Testing & Performance Engineering in CI' chapter to save a slot — note that option for the author.
- **What is missing / scope notes:** Already covered (point to, don't repeat): golden-capture replay with bit-identical assertion as the determinism gate (74/75/76), stress+invariants/fuzzing/model-checking for concurrency (40/72). Missing and in scope: a concrete test architecture for apply(state,input) with a scripted input log and expected outputs; table-driven order-book tests on the Ch. 25 case study with expected BBO/depth after each message; property-based tests (RapidCheck) with invariants such as book consistency, sequence monotonicity, conservation of quantity; deterministic simulation testing — a single-threaded simulated clock/network/venue with seeded fault injection (drops, reorders, late fills, rejects, disconnects) exploring many seeds, FoundationDB-style; test doubles for the impure shell without hot-path cost (clock/NIC/venue as template policies vs virtual seams — the Ch. 19 vs Ch. 14 trade, verified in asm per Ch. 4); cutting golden-master cases from captures (Ch. 75) and keeping them small; running the suite under the sanitizers (Ch. 40) and on ARM. Measure: that the production build with policy-injected doubles compiles to the same hot-path asm as the real one.
- **Closest existing coverage:**
  - 74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.2.2 (lines 41–60): the apply(state,input) functional-core model and its testability claim; §74.5 line 142 and §74.6 exercise 2/checklist: 'replay a captured session and assert bit-identical outputs'
  - 75-capture-persistence-replay-storage.md §75.4.5 (line 109), exercise 5, checklist: golden capture + bit-identical replay as a CI gate
  - 76-production-profiling-end-to-end-case-study.md §76.4.1 (lines 69–70): golden capture + golden output as the regression fixture; correctness gate
  - 40-concurrency-correctness-tooling.md §40.4.1 (stress testing with invariant checks), §40.4.2 (coverage-guided fuzzing of ITCH/OUCH/FIX/SBE parsers, differential fuzzing), §40.4.3 (model checking SPSC/seqlock with GenMC/CDSChecker/Relacy)
  - 72-secure-programming-low-latency.md §72.4.8: libFuzzer/AFL++ on the decode path under ASan/UBSan
- **Evidence (greps and reads):** Greps across chapters/*.md: 'unit.test|gtest|googletest|catch2|doctest|ctest' → 0 (only cyclictest hits); 'TEST\(|EXPECT_|ASSERT_EQ|REQUIRE\(|CHECK\(|\bassert\(' → 0 files — no test code anywhere in the book; 'property.based|rapidcheck|quickcheck|proptest' → 0; 'deterministic simulation|simulation test|FoundationDB|Jepsen|\bloom\b|fault.inject|chaos' → 0; '\bmock|test double|\bfake\b|\bstub\b|injectable|TimeSource' → 0 meaningful (one 'fake dependencies' in Ch. 11 about register renaming); 'table.driven' → 13-branch-prediction code comment only; 'golden|bit.identical' → 74 §74.2.2/§74.5/exercise 2/checklist ('replay a captured session and assert bit-identical outputs'), 75 §75.4.5 line 109, exercise 5, checklist, 76 §76.4.1 lines 69–70 (golden capture + correctness gate), §76.6 exercise 2. Opened Ch. 40 §40.4.1–40.4.3: stress testing (with one bullet 'property/invariant checks under stress'), fuzzing of parsers, model checking of lock-free primitives — all dynamic/concurrency-focused, no unit-test or property-based framework, no DST. Opened Ch. 74 §74.2.2: defines apply(state, input) → (state', outputs) and says the core is 'pure, testable, and replayable' but the only test shown is replay-equals-golden. Ch. 25 case study: no tests of the order book ('test' appears once, about benchmark working-set size).

## G61 — Performance engineering in CI: benchmarks as tests, A/A noise-floor calibration, deterministic proxies (retired instructions, cachegrind) for shared runners, statistical gates on p99/p99.9, automated bisection; operating the PGO/AutoFDO/BOLT profile lifecycle (collection on replayed captures, merging, staleness and validity gates)

- **Theme:** G. Engineering lifecycle · **Proposed kind:** new-chapter · **Sources:** sw-engineering#2, sw-engineering#10
- **Verdict:** partially-covered · **Priority:** high
- **Why this priority:** The book's own thesis that 'performance is a state you defend' rests on a gate it never shows how to build; a flapping p99.9 gate on a shared runner is the common failure mode for teams operating a tick-to-trade system, and the PGO staleness it warns about has no remedy in the text.
- **Refined placement:** Split as the sources suggest, with one refinement. (a) The CI performance mechanics deserve a new chapter in Part XII immediately before Ch. 76 — 'Performance Engineering in CI: benchmarks as tests, noise-floor calibration, deterministic proxies, statistical gates and automated bisection' — with Ch. 76 §76.4.1 shrunk to the principles plus a pointer, so the capstone keeps its walkthrough. Expanding §76.4.1 in place was considered and rejected: ~2,000 words of statistics and pipeline mechanics would swamp a 3,400-word capstone. If the G60 testing chapter is adopted, merging both into a single 'Testing & Performance Engineering in CI' chapter is a reasonable footprint-saving alternative. (b) The PGO/AutoFDO/BOLT profile lifecycle belongs in Ch. 22 as new §22.4.4 'Operating PGO/BOLT: the profile lifecycle' after §22.4.3 (it is a build-toolchain operation, not a CI-statistics topic), with the commands added to Appendix D §D.6 and Appendix I §I.9. (c) Appendix L gains §L.5 'CI skeleton' (ctest/labels, --benchmark_out=json, compare.py invocation, baseline artifact layout).
- **What is missing / scope notes:** Already covered: why a gate must compare distributions on a quiet box (76 §76.4.1, 3 §3.4.4), compare.py/Mann–Whitney named once, L.3/L.4 single-run hygiene, PGO/BOLT what-and-why with the staleness warning. Missing: A/A (same-binary) runs to calibrate the noise floor before setting any tolerance; bootstrap confidence intervals on p99/p99.9 rather than means; per-benchmark baselines stored as artifacts keyed by revision; deterministic proxies for shared runners (perf stat instructions:u/branches:u, cachegrind, llvm-mca) and when a proxy is allowed to stand in for wall time; per-PR fast gate vs nightly full replay gate on the tuned box; automated bisection driven by the replay harness; alert/trend tooling. Profile lifecycle: instrumented-build flags (-fprofile-generate / -fprofile-instr-generate, -fprofile-update=atomic cost), training on a captured replay including the open, %p/%m naming, llvm-profdata merge / gcov-tool merge and weighting, storing profiles by source revision, staleness/validity gates (-Wmissing-profile, -Wcoverage-mismatch, -fprofile-correction, -fprofile-partial-training), measuring a stale profile's pessimization (ties Ch. 22 exercise 5), AutoFDO refresh cadence from production perf, BOLT perf2bolt on the shipped binary.
- **Closest existing coverage:**
  - 76-production-profiling-end-to-end-case-study.md §76.4.1 (lines 65–73): the gate in principle — golden fixture, bit-identical correctness gate, p99.9-vs-baseline latency gate with tolerance, 'stable measurement environment… compare distributions', trend dashboard; §76.4.2 line 81: bisection by replay (one bullet); §76.5 line 98: the noisy-gate pitfall; exercises 2 and 4
  - 03-microbenchmarking-done-right.md §3.4.4 (lines 244–252): repetitions/stddev, sample counts for a quoted percentile, compare.py + Mann–Whitney (one bullet), 'gate regressions in CI' (one bullet); exercise 5
  - appendix-L-benchmark-harness.md §L.3 (repeatable environment: pinning, governor, NUMA, perf stat, metadata) and §L.4 (pre-trust checklist incl. 'effect above noise')
  - appendix-I-tooling-cookbook.md §I.7 (lines 117–123): cachegrind as a deterministic cross-check — one line
  - 22-build-toolchain-for-speed.md §22.4.3 (lines 117–120), §22.5 (line 128), exercise 5, checklist; 12-instruction-cache-frontend-code-layout.md §12.4.4, §12.6 (line 261): PGO/AutoFDO/BOLT and the stale/unrepresentative-profile warning — no procedure
  - 40-concurrency-correctness-tooling.md §40.5 (lines 137–139): sanitizer builds as CI jobs gating merges
- **Evidence (greps and reads):** Greps across chapters/*.md: 'A/A|noise floor|noise threshold|null test' → 0 meaningful; 'compare\.py|mann.whitney|u.test' → 03-microbenchmarking-done-right.md §3.4.4 line 250 (one bullet: compare.py applies Mann–Whitney U), exercise 5 line 277, references line 297; 'bootstrap|kolmogorov|KS test|confidence interval|p.value|welch' → 0; 'cachegrind|instruction count|retired instruction|deterministic proxy' → appendix-I-tooling-cookbook.md §I.7 lines 117–123 (cachegrind one-liner, 'deterministic cross-check when hardware counters are noisy'), Ch. 2/4/8 use instruction counts only to argue 'same work, different cycles'; 'nightly|GitHub Actions|Jenkins|Buildkite' → 0; 'regression gate|bisect|\bCI\b' → 76 §76.4.1 lines 65–73 (five bullets: golden fixture, correctness gate, latency gate 'against a baseline with a tolerance', stable environment, track the trend), §76.4.2 line 81 (one bullet on replay-based bisection), §76.5 line 98 (noisy-gate pitfall), exercises 2 and 4; 3 §3.4.4 line 252 ('store its baseline and fail the build… built out in Ch. 76'); 40 §40.5 lines 137–139 (sanitizer builds in CI, TSan/ASan separate jobs); 72 §72.4.8 (fuzzing in CI). Profile lifecycle: 'llvm-profdata|profdata|profraw|\.gcda|gcov|missing-profile|coverage-mismatch|profile-update|LLVM_PROFILE_FILE' → 0; 'stale|representative profile|autofdo|fprofile' → 22 §22.4.3 lines 117–120 (three-step PGO, AutoFDO, BOLT order, 'refresh profiles as the strategy/code changes'), §22.5 line 128 (stale-profile pitfall), exercise 5, checklist; 12 §12.4.4 lines 196–200, §12.6 line 261 — warnings only, no collection/merge/storage/validity mechanics. Opened Appendix L §L.3/§L.4: environment setup and pre-trust checklist for a single run, nothing on CI integration.

## G62 — Static analysis, performance linters and code review for the hot path, plus performance contracts in code: clang-tidy performance-* checks, custom checks for the book's rules, static_assert layout/lock-freedom/noexcept tables, link-time no-syscall/no-malloc assertions

- **Theme:** G. Engineering lifecycle · **Proposed kind:** new-chapter · **Sources:** sw-engineering#4, sw-engineering#5
- **Verdict:** partially-covered · **Priority:** high
- **Why this priority:** The book teaches dozens of hot-path rules enforced only by vigilance and runtime tools; static enforcement and a consolidated performance-contract pattern are central to building the system with a team, and the contracts half is an author-requested design-patterns area.
- **Refined placement:** Two pieces. (1) Static analysis / linters / review: a new chapter is justified because the tooling is entirely absent and spans every rule in the book; place it in Part III as the closing chapter after Ch. 22 (Build Toolchain for Speed) — 'Enforcing the Hot-Path Rules: clang-tidy, custom checks, review checklists and link-time assertions' — since Part III is where the compiler/toolchain is made to work for latency, and the chapter is the static complement to Ch. 40's dynamic tools (add it to Ch. 40's 'Leads into'). If the separately proposed build-systems chapter (sw-engineering#3) is adopted, place it directly after that instead. Part I after Ch. 5 is a weaker fit: readers there lack the rules the checks enforce. Appendix D §D.7 and Appendix I §I.9 gain pointers and the clang-tidy/clang-query/--wrap one-liners. (2) Performance contracts: new §18.4.3 'Performance contracts: asserting latency invariants at build time' in Ch. 18, expanding the single 'compile-time validation' bullet and consolidating the scattered pieces (Ch. 9 layout table, Ch. 30 is_always_lock_free, Ch. 33 interference size, Ch. 34/37 power-of-two, Ch. 20 noexcept, Ch. 53 wire overlays, Ch. 50 trivially-copyable shm slots), with back-references from those sections and a one-page consolidated table in Appendix D or I.
- **What is missing / scope notes:** Already covered (reuse, don't repeat): static_assert layout pins (Ch. 9), runtime operator-new counting (Ch. 23), strace/bpftrace syscall hunts (Ch. 41/61), noexcept as contract (Ch. 20), warning flags and sanitizers (D.7). Missing for the chapter: clang-tidy performance-* checks (unnecessary-copy-initialization, for-range-copy, unnecessary-value-param, move-const-arg, inefficient-vector-operation, no-automatic-move, noexcept-move-constructor) plus the relevant bugprone-*/cppcoreguidelines-*/misc-const-correctness, compile_commands.json, pinned tidy version in CI, suppression policy; measuring what a flagged copy actually costs (Ch. 3) so the linter is calibrated, not dogma; custom clang-tidy/clang-query checks encoding the book's rules (no virtual/std::function/new/throw/syscall in functions annotated hot, structs annotated hot fit a line); a hot-path code-review checklist drawn from each chapter's checklist; link-time enforcement: -Wl,--wrap=malloc/operator new resolving to a trap in the hot-path TU, nm/objdump scans for malloc/syscall symbols in hot sections, seccomp strict mode as a runtime no-syscall assertion in test builds. For §18.4.3: the layout contract (sizeof/alignof/offsetof, is_trivially_copyable/is_standard_layout for shm/memcpy'd types, hardware_destructive_interference_size pinned to 64), the concurrency contract (is_always_lock_free, power-of-two capacities), the exception contract (static_assert(noexcept(expr)) tables), and the constant-folding contract (consteval/if consteval to prove a table folded).
- **Closest existing coverage:**
  - 09-object-layout-alignment-padding.md §9.3 (lines 101–105): static_assert(sizeof/alignof/offsetof) tables with code; §9.6 (lines 214, 219): layout and wire-struct pitfalls; checklist line 237
  - 18-compile-time-mechanics.md §18.4.1 (line 135): 'compile-time validation' bullet (static_assert/consteval); §18.5 line 176; checklist line 201
  - 30-cpp-memory-model-atomics.md §30.6 (line 175) and checklist (line 197): confirm is_always_lock_free for hot-path atomics; 33 §33.5 (line 147): padding can break is_lock_free/ABI
  - 23-memory-management-fundamentals.md §23.5 (line 135), exercise 2 (line 150), checklist (line 157): override global operator new to count/abort to prove zero allocation — runtime, not link-time; 38 §38.1 and exercise 2 apply it to coroutine frames
  - 41-context-switching-mitigation.md exercise 5 (line 158) and 61 §61.4.1 (line 86): strace -c / bpftrace syscall audit of a 'syscall-free' loop — runtime
  - 20-the-cost-of-abstractions.md §20.2 (line 49), §20.4.1 (lines 139–140): noexcept as the documented happy-path contract
  - appendix-D-compiler-flag-reference.md §D.7: -Wpadded, -Wfloat-equal ('code review'), -Wall -Wextra, -fopt-info, sanitizers; appendix-I §I.9: objdump/nm/sanitizer one-liners
- **Evidence (greps and reads):** Greps across chapters/*.md: 'clang-tidy|clang.query|cppcheck|coverity|pvs-studio|linter|\blint\b|clang-format|include-what' → 0; 'static analy' → only the eBPF verifier (61 §61.2); 'code review|review checklist' → appendix-D §D.7 line 73 ('-Wfloat-equal… useful in price/numeric code review') — one phrase; '-Werror|coding standard|core guidelines' → Core Guidelines/CERT appear only as references in 18, 19, 20, 72, G; 'static_assert' → 09-object-layout (12 occurrences; §9.3 lines 101–105 develop static_assert(sizeof/alignof/offsetof) with code, §9.6 line 219 'wire structs without static_assert(offsetof/sizeof)' pitfall, checklist line 237), 18-compile-time (3: §18.4.1 line 135 'compile-time validation' bullet, §18.5 line 176, checklist line 201), 34/37 (power-of-two capacity asserts in code); 'is_always_lock_free|is_lock_free' → 30 §30.6 line 175 and checklist line 197, 33 §33.5 line 147 and checklist; 'wrap=|--wrap|nm .*malloc|LD_PRELOAD|operator new.*(count|abort)' → 23 §23.5 line 135, exercise 2 line 150, checklist line 157 ('override operator new to count/abort — don't eyeball it': a runtime instrument), 38 §38.1/exercise 2 (instrument operator new for coroutine frames), LD_PRELOAD only as Onload (55/62); 'strace.*hot|no.syscall' → 41 exercise 5 line 158 (strace -c hunt for hidden syscalls), 61 §61.4.1 line 86 (bpftrace syscall audit); noexcept-as-contract → 20 §20.2 line 49, §20.4.1 lines 139–140 (conditional noexcept, don't lie). Opened Appendix D.7 and I.9: warnings, -fopt-info, sanitizers, objdump/nm/BOLT one-liners; no static-analysis tool, no link-time assertion. Conclusion: the contract *ingredients* exist scattered (layout asserts developed in Ch. 9, lock-free check as a checklist item, runtime operator-new counting), but no static-analysis tooling, no custom checks, no review checklist, no link-time no-malloc/no-syscall proof, and no consolidated contract pattern.

## G63 — Release engineering for trading systems: deploy calendar as a latency decision, blue/green and hot-standby cutover for process-per-role, rollback, canary shards and shadow runs, venue conformance; measuring latency changes in production (canary hosts, shadow box fed by layer-1 replication, paired comparison on identical ticks)

- **Theme:** G. Engineering lifecycle · **Proposed kind:** new-chapter · **Sources:** sw-engineering#7, health-app#7, observability-depth#7
- **Verdict:** partially-covered · **Priority:** high
- **Why this priority:** Operating a tick-to-trade system means changing it almost daily without dropping a tick; the book covers in-process reload and crash recovery but has no procedure for shipping a new binary, rolling back, or trusting a build before it trades — and health-app#7 is in the author-requested health-monitoring-app area.
- **Refined placement:** A new chapter is justified: Part XII after Ch. 74 (Process Topology) and before Ch. 75 (Capture) — 'Release Engineering for Trading Systems: deploy windows, standby cutover, rollback, canary shards, shadow runs and venue conformance'. It consumes Ch. 73's reload, Ch. 74's supervisor/sharding/warm restart and Ch. 46's side-effect suppression, and the Ch. 50 versioned-layout contract (and G64's compatibility window) for attaching a new process version to live rings. A §74.4.6 in Ch. 74 was considered and rejected: Ch. 74 is already 4,400 words and this is a process, not a topology mechanism. The production paired-comparison piece (observability-depth#7) should live in Ch. 76 as new §76.4.4 'From CI gate to production: canary hosts and paired comparison on identical ticks' — it extends §76.4.1's gate and answers §76.5's drift pitfall — with a cross-reference from the new chapter; Ch. 73 §73.4.4 and Appendix C §C.5 (readiness gate) and Appendix K §K.4 (conformance) get one-line pointers.
- **What is missing / scope notes:** Already covered (point to): in-process config/strategy cutover and warm-up (73 §73.4.4), warm restart and sharding (74 §74.4.4–74.4.5), side-effect-suppressed shadow traffic (46 §46.4.1), signed artifacts (72 §72.4.7), -march fleet pitfalls (22). Missing for the chapter: the deploy calendar as a latency decision (what may change intraday via Ch. 73 vs only in pre-open/post-close/weekend windows — binary, kernel, NIC firmware, BIOS; change freezes around expiries/macro events); blue/green or hot-standby cutover for process-per-role (start vN+1 beside vN on reserved cores, attach to the data planes under a compatibility window, warm it, flip the gateway's accepted writer, keep vN for instant rollback, measuring the cutover's tick-loss and tail); rollback mechanics incl. state compatibility; canary shards (promote on one symbol/venue shard under tighter risk limits, compare per-shard SLIs); shadow runs with decision parity (candidate on the same shm ring with no gateway credentials, parity rate/timing/divergence budget/auto-stop); venue conformance and certification sessions and weekend DR tests; the pre-trading-day readiness gate as a procedure. For Ch. 76 §76.4.4: a shadow host fed by layer-1 replication (Ch. 60) running the candidate with TX suppressed, paired per-tick latency differences on bit-identical input, bootstrap CI on the paired p99.9 delta, and why production-only effects (DDIO/neighbour contention, real microbursts, venue behaviour) make the CI gate necessary but insufficient.
- **Closest existing coverage:**
  - 73-hot-reload-live-reconfiguration.md §73.4.3 (validate-before-swap) and §73.4.4 (lines 91–97): in-process instantaneous vs draining cutover; dlopen strategy .so reload with warm-up and safe reclaim — the one release angle that exists (strategy code, in-process)
